IOT-BASED SMART PEST DETECTION IN AGRICULTURE

Smart pest detection in agriculture using IoT (the Internet of Things) involves the integration of sensor technologies, data analytics, and connectivity to monitor and detect pests in agricultural fields. It aims to provide real-time information about pest infestations, enabling farmers to take timely and targeted action to mitigate the damage and protect their crops. Here is an overview of how intelligent pest detection in agriculture using IoT works:

Sensor Deployment

Various sensors are strategically placed in agricultural fields to collect data related to environmental conditions such as temperature, humidity, soil moisture, and light intensity [35]. These sensors can be connected wirelessly to a central IoT gateway or network.

Pest Detection Sensors

In addition to environmental sensors, specialized pest detection sensors can be used to monitor the presence of pests. These sensors can detect specific signs of pest activity, such as pheromones, vibrations, or acoustic signals generated by pests.

Data Collection and Transmission

The sensor data is collected and transmitted to a central hub or cloud-based platform using wireless communication protocols such as Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, or LoRaWAN. The data may include information about environmental conditions, pest activity, and other relevant parameters.

Data Analytics and Processing

The collected data is analyzed using machine learning algorithms or other data processing techniques to identify patterns, correlations, and anomalies related to pest infestations. This analysis can help differentiate between normal environmental variations and signs of pest presence.

Pest Detection and Alerts

Based on the data analysis, the system can generate real-time alerts or notifications to farmers, indicating the presence of pests or potential pest outbreaks. These alerts can be delivered through mobile applications, SMS, email, or other communication channels.

Decision Support System

The smart pest detection system can provide farmers with actionable insights and recommendations on pest control measures. It may suggest appropriate pest management strategies, such as applying specific pesticides or deploying natural predators.

Integration with other Agricultural Systems

Intelligent pest detection systems can be integrated with other agricultural technologies, such as irrigation or automated machinery. This integration allows for more efficient and targeted pest control measures, reducing the use of pesticides and optimizing resource allocation.

Historical Data Analysis and Prediction

Over time, the system can accumulate a valuable database of pest-related information. Analyzing historical data makes it possible to identify recurring patterns and trends, enabling predictive models for future pest outbreaks. This helps farmers proactively plan and implement preventive measures.

Benefits of Smart Pest Detection in Agriculture using IoT Include

  • Early detection of pest infestations allows farmers to take prompt action and minimize crop damage.
  • Targeted pest control measures by reducing the use of pesticides and minimizing their environmental impact.
  • Optimized resource allocation, as farmers can focus their efforts on the areas most affected by pests.
  • Improved crop yield and quality through proactive pest management strategies.
  • It enhances decision-making capabilities by leveraging real-time and historical data.
  • It increases operational efficiency by integrating pest detection systems with other agricultural technologies.

PROBLEM STATEMENT

When farmers use excessive chemical fertilizers and pesticides, crop quality suffers and soil quality declines. The proposed smart agriculture system aims to monitor crops in real-time, identify pests in the field and implement decision-support advisory models. Additionally, mobile devices allow remote control of various functions in the field.

EXISTING METHOD

A common crop serves as an illustration for the pest identification procedure. The first step is to gather the damaged crops for analysis. Several earlier researches made use of a median filter for noise reduction. The value of a pixel is determined by the pixels in the neighboring region, with the median value or average being used in some instances.

Image Acquisition

Leaf images were captured from the crop fields by using a high-resolution digital wireless camera network that is capable of withstanding extreme weather conditions. The most advanced method uses a CISCO Linksys Wireless-G Internet Home Monitoring Camera. The camera's 8-megapixel resolution allows for 10-fps recording, and the photos can be processed locally on a PC with 4 GB RAM and an Intel i3 CPU.

Pre-processing

The first step in the pre-processing is the removal of the noise. Several earlier researches made use of a median filter for noise reduction. The value of a pixel is determined by the pixel’s values in the neighboring region, with the median value or average being used in some instances. The given dataset is resized and cropped into the required dimensions. The image is then converted into an HSV model or a grayscale one (I(x, y) = 0.2989R + 0.5870G + 0.1140B) from the RGB model based on the required application.

Segmentation: Based on specific attributes, clustering of the images is done. Segmentation technique is used to detect the presence of pests. It plays an important role in identifying the difference between the pest and other parts of the image. Various threshold techniques like entropy-based threshold and histogram-based threshold are generally used.

Feature extraction

Based on the selected clusters, various features such as Mean, Standard deviation (SD), Entropy, RMS, Variance, Smoothness, Kurtosis, Skewness, Contrast, Correlation, Energy, and homogeneity are calculated by using GLMC (Gray Level Co-occurrence Matrix), which will help to find the pest and the percentage of the affected area.

Classification

Supervised machine learning methods like SVM (Support Vector Machine) can be used to examine data and identify similar types of patterns that are used for the later classification.

PROPOSED MODEL

The primary goal of the proposed system is to identify plant leaf diseases using feature extraction techniques that take form, color, and texture into account. Plant leaves are classified as healthy or diseased using the convolutional neural network (CNN), a machine learning approach. If the plant leaf is diseased, CNN will identify the ailment [36]. It is made to suggest treatments for certain diseases that will aid in the growth of healthy plants and increase output. To get better outcomes and efficiency, high resolution camera is first used to capture photographs of diverse leaves. Afterward, these images are subjected to image processing techniques in order to extract valuable features that are necessary for further investigation.

The manual inspection required by conventional methods of plant disease identification can be time-consuming, subjective, and error-prone. Accurately identifying and categorizing various diseases is the aim of employing a convolutional neural network (CNN) for disease detection in plant leaves. The effectiveness and precision of disease detection can be greatly increased by using a CNN for plant leaf disease detection. A reliable and unbiased evaluation of the plant's health can be obtained using a CNN-based system because it can swiftly and reliably analyze massive amounts of data. The overall goal of deploying a CNN for plant leaf disease detection is to enable early and accurate disease identification, which can assist farmers and plant scientists in timely and appropriate disease prevention.

Plant leaves can be analyzed using a CNN-based system to look for disease indicators, including spots, discoloration, and deformation. The severity of the condition may also be determined using this technology, which can also assist in identifying the exact disease-causing agent, whether it be a virus, bacteria, or fungus.

The proposed system captures the images via Arduino, which are then stored in the dataset. Later, the image dataset is analyzed based on the CNN approach i.e., identify, categorize, and detect the diseases. Then the data is processed, and decision making is done for recommended precision or a fertilizer, which is displayed in the product result of the Android app through the internet, as shown in Fig. (1). The Internet of Things (IoT) and the Android operating system are the basis for this architecture. IoT is used to aid the procedure. The approach is helpful for farmers since it facilitates lawful harvest expansion.

Fig. (1))
Proposed system architecture.

Proposed Methodology

Fig. (2) shows the step-by-step implementation of the proposed system architecture. Different libraries like pandas, NUMPY, OS, mat plot lib and tensor flow were used in order to execute this process.

Fig. (2))
Process flow of CNN machine learning approach.

Data Collection

The dataset of healthy and unhealthy corn leaf images were collected from Kaggle. This dataset consisted of 4000 images of the corn leaves. Then, the images acquired were classified and labeled as a) Gray spot leaves, b) Blight spot leaves, c) Rust leaves, and d) Healthy leaves.

Dataset Augmentation

Dataset augmentation Overfitting may be a significant issue when training neural networks because it happens when a model grows too complicated and begins to fit too closely to the training data, resulting in poor generalization to new, unknown data. Data augmentation is indeed a powerful technique that is used to tackle overfitting. By applying transformations such as rotations, translations, scaling, flips, and other distortions to the existing training data, we can artificially increase the diversity of the data seen by the model during training. This process helps the model to learn more robust features and reduces its reliance on specific details or noise present in the training set alone. In practice, data augmentation is widely used in tasks such as image classification, object detection, and natural language processing, among others. It helps improve the overall performance and reliability of machine learning models, making them more suitable for real-world applications where the data may vary in ways that are not fully represented in the initial training set.

Classification of Datasets

The dataset is divided into training, validation, and testing sets for machine learning tasks.

Training Set: This set is used to train the machine learning model. 70% of the total images are allocated to the training set.

Validation Set: This set is used to tune hyperparameters and evaluate models during training without influencing the model training itself. 20% of the images are allocated to the validation set.

Testing Set: This set is used to evaluate the final performance of the trained model. It is used only after the model has been trained and tuned using the training and validation sets. 10% of the images are allocated to the testing set.

Deep Learning Model

For classification purposes, CNN has been used. A typical CNN architecture consists of input, convolutional, and pooling layers, followed by fully connected layers at the end [37]. Activation functions are applied after convolutional and fully connected layers to introduce non-linearity, enabling the network to learn complex mappings from the input data to the output labels. This architecture is well-suited for tasks such as image classification, object detection, and image segmentation, where the spatial relationships and hierarchical patterns in the data are crucial for accurate predictions. However, as neural networks grow deeper, they encounter difficulties in training due to vanishing gradients. However, Residual Networks (ResNets) overcome the vanishing gradient problem and have been shown to achieve better performance and accuracy compared to shallower networks and other deep architectures in various tasks, especially in image classification, object detection, and segmentation. Therefore, in this work, ResNet is used for the detection of the disease.

ResNet Architecture: ResNet has shown superior performance in the ImageNet Large Scale Visual Recognition Challenge (ILSVRC), with an error rate of 3.57%, showcasing its superiority in image classification, detection, and localization tasks. Residual blocks are a fundamental component of Residual Neural Networks (ResNets). The components of a residual block consist of a Convolution Block and an Identity Block. Fig. (3) shows the components of the residual block.

Fig. (3))
Components of residual block.

Convolutional block: It is used when the input and output dimensions of the block differ, requiring the convolutional layers to adjust the feature map dimensions. The skip connection ensures gradient flow and facilitates the learning of residual mappings for complex transformations.

Identity Block: It is used when the input and output dimensions of the block are the same, allowing the block to act as an identity function. The skip connection maintains the original information flow and facilitates easier training of very deep networks by preserving gradient flow.

ResNet-18 is a specific variant of the Residual Network (ResNet) architecture, known for its relative simplicity compared to deeper versions like ResNet-50 or ResNet-101. Hence, in this work, ResNet 18 is used. Fig. (4) shows the architecture of ResNet 18.

Fig. (4))
RESNET18 Architecture.

Structure of ResNet-18

Initial Layers

Convolutional Layer: ResNet-18 begins with a standard convolutional layer that processes the input image data. This layer typically uses a 7x7 kernel with 64 filters, followed by batch normalization and ReLU activation.

Max Pooling: Following the initial convolutional layer, a max pooling layer with a 3x3 kernel and stride of 2 downsamples the spatial dimensions of the feature maps.

Basic Blocks (Residual Blocks)

ResNet-18 is composed of several residual blocks, which are the building blocks of the architecture. Each residual block consists of two convolutional layers with batch normalization and ReLU activation.

Specifically:

First Block: Two 3x3 convolutional layers with 64 filters each.

Second Block: Two 3x3 convolutional layers with 128 filters each.

Third Block: Two 3x3 convolutional layers with 256 filters each.

Fourth Block: Two 3x3 convolutional layers with 512 filters each.

Global Average Pooling and Classifier

  • After the series of residual blocks, global average pooling is applied to reduce the spatial dimensions of the feature maps to a vector.
  • The pooled features are then fed into a fully connected (FC) layer, followed by a softmax activation function for classification tasks. This final layer produces probabilities for each class.

Training and Testing the Model

In this step, the model is built using the above architecture, and the custom-built layers are trained with the training dataset available. During the training, the model tries to recognize and understand the underlying features and patterns in the training dataset. The weights are assigned to the custom-built layers of the model during the training phase. These weights are used to classify whether the given image is affected by blight, gray, rust, and black spots. Thus, the built model is first compiled, and then it is pipelined into the training phase using the ‘model.fit’ function in the keras module.

Performance Evaluation

The performance of the model is evaluated based on the confusion matrix, Accuracy, Precision, Recall, and F1-score parameters are discussed in the results and discussions section.

Components Used

Arduino UNO

Using an Arduino UNO microcontroller board in an innovative pest detection system in agriculture can provide a cost-effective and flexible solution. It can interface with various sensors used for pest detection, such as temperature, humidity, light, vibration, or specialized pest detection sensors. These sensors are connected to the appropriate digital or analog input pins on the Arduino UNO. Sensor data is read using the Arduino UNO's analog or digital input pins. Proper libraries or codes are used to interface with the sensors and retrieve the sensor readings. The Arduino UNO's processing capabilities are utilized to analyze the sensor data. Algorithms or logic are applied to identify patterns or anomalies related to pest infestations. Arduino UNO can communicate with other devices or systems to relay collected data or receive instructions. It can use different communication interfaces like Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, or GSM modules, depending on the requirements. The appropriate communication module is connected to transmit the data wirelessly to a gateway or via the internet to a remote server. An alert system is implemented using Arduino UNO to notify farmers about detected pests. It can use LED indicators, sound buzzers, or LCDs to indicate the presence of pests or trigger specific actions. For example, when pests are detected, the Arduino UNO can activate an alarm or send an alert message via SMS.

The Arduino board shown in Fig. (5) is an ATmega328P-based microcontroller. It operates at 16 MHz. The 16 MHz quartz crystal provides the clock signal for the ATmega328P microcontroller, ensuring accurate timing for operations. It features a total of 14 digital I/O pins and includes a USB port and a power jack.

Fig. (5))
Arduino board.

Relay

Relays can be integrated into smart pest detection systems in agriculture to enable automated actions based on the detected pest activity. When the intelligent pest detection system identifies the presence of pests through sensor data analysis, it can send a signal to activate the relay. The relay can be connected to various devices or systems to trigger automated actions. The relay can activate or adjust the irrigation system to wash away or disturb the pests, as certain pests may be vulnerable to water. If pests are detected, the relay can activate sprinklers to deter or disperse the pests. In cases where pesticide application is necessary, the relay can control the spraying mechanism, starting it only in the areas affected by pests. The relay can control the deployment of traps or barriers, such as sticky or physical barriers, to capture or prevent pests from reaching the crops.

RESULTS AND DISCUSSIONS

The performance of the model is evaluated by calculating parameters like confusion matrix, accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score of test data images. The confusion matrix is able to assess the performance of convolution neural network models with minimal loss. The values of these parameters can be calculated by predictions, which are classified into four categories: 1) True Positive(TP), 2) True Negative (TN), 3) False positive (FP), and 4)False negative(FN). TP indicates that the model predicts both the predicted class and actual class to be true. TN indicates that the model predicts both the predicted class and the actual class to be negative. FP indicates that the model predicts actual classes as negative and predicted classes as positive. FN indicates actual class as negative and predicted as positive. Accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score can be calculated by using the following formulas:

(4)

Few blight leaves are incorrectly classified as common rust and grey spot leaves. The classifier accuracy, which was achieved on the training and testing dataset, was 99%.

CONCLUSION

The planned study used different types of corn leaves to detect the diseases using Convolution Neural Networks. To classify the patterns of the corn leaf, ResNet18 was used. The proposed CNN model is able to detect various kinds of corn leaf diseases such as healthy leaf, gray leaf, rust leaf, and blight leaf. From the results, it was observed that the classifier accuracy was 99%.As a result, this model will be a valuable resource for farmers to use when making informed choices about their crops at an early stage.

FUTURE WORK

Diseases like eye spots, Goss's wilt, brown spots, etc., cannot be detected with this model. There is a chance of detecting these diseases by introducing improved deep learning models in the future. In addition, future research should focus on expanding and refining the data collection covering all crop kinds and associated illnesses.

Weed Control for Better Crop Health Using AIoT



Sanjana T.1, *, Madhusudhan K. N.1
1 B.M.S. College of Engineering, Bull Temple Rd, Basavanagudi, Bengaluru, Karnataka-560019, India

Abstract

Agriculture is one of the major sources of economy in India. Quality of crops and optimal yield are possible amidst several challenges, such as climate change, water scarcity, and weeds, by means of sustainable agriculture. Modern science and technological advancements can be used to address these challenges and, hence, maximize agricultural productivity.

This chapter focuses on weed control using a combination of computer vision and IoT. Weed is an unwanted crop that affects the growth of the actual crop by absorbing soil nutrients, water, and sunlight. It is extremely important to remove the weeds. Targeted spraying to kill the weed has been the most effective solution. Such a system is cost-effective and time-saving for large farms. The major concern in this method is the identification of the weed. There are several types of weeds, and identifying them among the actual crop is critical. False identification may lead to large economic losses. An efficient product for weed removal can be designed by combining the knowledge of IoT, image processing, and artificial intelligence (AI). AI and image processing aid in identifying and classifying the weeds. AI also helps in analyzing the risk of weed and the ineffective usage of weed killer, along with the amount of pesticide to be sprayed based on the type of weed. This chapter discusses sustainable agriculture, works carried out in the field of smart farming, the significance of technologies such as IoT and AI, and the design of a weed killer bot, which mainly uses image processing.

Keywords: Agriculture, Artificial intelligence, Bot, Crop identification, Image segmentation, Internet of Things, Precise targeting, Prototype, Sustainability, Weed.

* Corresponding author Sanjana T.: B.M.S. College of Engineering, Bull Temple Rd, Basavanagudi, Bengaluru, Karnataka-560019; Tel: 9964113686; E-mail: sanjanat.ece@bmsce.ac.in

INTRODUCTION

In India, agriculture is the primary source of livelihood for millions of people. India is one of the world's largest producers of food grains, but its agriculture is

plagued by several challenges, such as a shortage of land, water scarcity, soil degradation, and climate change. Weed infestation adds to these challenges, making it difficult for agronomists to achieve optimal yields.

Sustainability in agriculture is crucial for ensuring long-term food security and protecting the environment. Sustainable agriculture practices promote the efficient use of natural resources, minimize environmental damage, and promote social and economic well-being.

Weed control is a critical aspect of agriculture, and the adoption of sustainable practices is essential to ensure long-term agricultural productivity and environmental health. By promoting sustainable weed management practices, farmers can maximize yields and agricultural productivity while minimizing the negative impacts of weed control on the environment and human health.

The concept of targeted is a relevant solution that mainly addresses the requirement of sustainable farming and control of weeds in agriculture.

This approach involves the precise application of herbicides only where weeds are present, reducing the amount of chemicals needed and minimizing the negative impact on non-target plants and the environment. By adopting this method, farmers can improve their crop yields while promoting sustainable agriculture practices that minimize environmental damage and reduce costs. Targeted spraying is a more efficient and environmentally friendly method of weed control, and it is a practical solution for agriculturalists who seek to improve their yield while maintaining sustainability in their farming practices.

Targeted weedicide spraying is an efficient and sustainable method of weed control compared to bulk spraying. Herbicides are consistently sprayed in a field during bulk spraying, which can lead to excessive use of chemicals, damage to non-target plants, and detrimental effects on the soil, water, and public health. Targeted spraying, on the other hand, allows for precise application of herbicides only where needed, resulting in a more effective and environmentally friendly weed control approach.

The targeted weedicide spraying mechanism involves the use of advanced technologies such as GPS, sensors, and mapping systems to identify and locate weeds within a field. Once the weeds are identified, the sprayer is directed only to the areas where the weeds are present, reducing the amount of herbicide needed and minimizing the impact on non-target plants and the environment. Targeted spraying can also save time and reduce costs, as farmers do not need to apply herbicides to the entire field.

There are several benefits of adopting targeted weedicide spraying. It reduces the amount of herbicide needed, resulting in cost savings for farmers and minimizing the negative impact on the environment. Targeted spraying also reduces the chances of survival of herbicide-resistant weeds. Additionally, it can improve crop yields by reducing the utilization of resources such as nutrients, water, and light by the weed.

Concisely, targeted weedicide spraying is an effective and sustainable method of weed control that can help farmers minimize the negative impacts of herbicides on the environment and improve crop productivity. By adopting targeted spraying mechanisms, farmers can reduce the use of chemicals, save time and money, and promote sustainable agriculture practices. The information collected from the farm using sensors can be sent to the farmer through IoT. An agricultural bot that combines IoT, image processing, and AI is the solution. It uses a camera, sensors, processor, motor, and sprayer. It effectively sprays the pesticide and kills the weed.

LITERATURE SURVEY

Precise identification of the unwanted vegetation is necessary for the development of a successful weed removal. Real-time weed detection system reconstructs a 3D model of the crop using stereo vision and machine learning to find weeds in the crop. Two manually compiled datasets of the cucumber and onion crop serve as the basis for the machine learning model's training in [1].

The machine learning models are trained using the ResNet-50 method and Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN). It was interpreted that ResNet-50 outperforms Convolution Neural Networks in terms of performance. For the dataset of cucumbers and for the dataset of onion crops the ResNet-50 model provides good accuracy.

In a study [2], authors used image processing in MATLAB to detect the area where there was the presence of weeds in an image we took from the fields. In recent years, as the world population has grown and existing land and natural resources have decreased, precision agriculture is increasingly capturing the attention of researchers. Image processing approaches can be applied to solve this problem.

The best ML algorithm to be used for achieving the said objective is a topic of discussion. The effectiveness of several machine learning methods, such as k-nearest neighbor (KNN), support vector machine (SVM), and random forest (RF), is examined in a study [3] to identify weeds using UAV photos taken from an Australian chili crop field. Accuracy, false positive rate recall, precision, and kappa coefficient are the evaluation criteria utilized to compare performance. The machine learning techniques are simulated in MATLAB, and the weed detection accuracies attained with RF are superior compared to SVM and KNN. According to this research, the RF and SVM algorithms are effective and practical to use, and they can be quickly deployed for weed detection in UAV images.

In contrast, a novel approach that combines deep learning with image processing technologies is proposed in a research work [4]. First, vegetables were identified, and bounding boxes were created around them using a trained CenterNet model. The remaining green things that continued to fall out of the surrounding boxes were then classified as weeds. By concentrating just on detecting vegetables in this way, the model may avoid dealing with different weed species. Additionally, this approach can significantly minimize the complexity of weed detection as well as the amount of the training image dataset, improving the performance and accuracy of weed identification. An image processing technique called color index-based segmentation was used to separate the weeds from the background. Using Genetic Algorithms (GAs), the employed color index was chosen and assessed in accordance with the Bayesian classification error. The trained CenterNet model obtained a good precision and recall test. The proposed index produces outstanding segmentation quality at a significantly lower computational cost. The findings of this experiment show that the suggested method can be utilized to identify weeds in vegetable plantations successfully on the ground.

For variable rate pesticide spraying, authors in a study [5] described a real-time computer vision-based crop/weed detection system. The Random Forest classifier was used to detect and categorize crops and weeds. With their custom dataset, the classification model was first offline trained before being put to the test in the field. The application equipment used for spraying agrochemicals has a PWM-based fluid flow control system that can spray precise amounts of agrochemicals as instructed by a vision-based feedback system. The outcomes of multiple field experiments show that the suggested vision-based agrochemical spraying framework is successful in real time.

In a study [6], authors deal with the weed detection technique, which uses image processing and CNN algorithm using the Keras model technique. Through this process, we can detect weeds so that we can spray herbicides only on the weed rather than spraying in the entire field, or we can directly pick it from their place of existence.

The effectiveness of the tube stamp method of weed removal in the case of organic farming with highly dense or closely spaced plants is discussed in a study [7]. This technique is especially helpful when the weed is closely spaced with the actual crop. The bot is controlled remotely through a web-based application. In a study, the authors [8] used structural features of leaves and image processing. The proposed algorithm generated a guiding line for the movement of autonomous bots across paddy fields. The algorithm showed good accuracy in terms of guiding the bot, which is of prime importance in weed control. This can be further improved for worse climatic conditions. Machine learning and computer vision algorithms are used in research work [9] to control the weeding vehicle, and the prototype also tries to aim at early-stage control of weeds. Web server, microcontroller, ESP8266, relay, motors for movement and cutting are used. In addition, a solar panel is used for powering up the components. The authors also indicated that the bot may be used for other tasks, such as soil testing and herbicide spraying. A detailed comparison of robotic systems and conventional systems is provided in a study [10] with respect to the size of the field, economic and environmental factors, power and fuel consumption, and cost per hour. It has been shown that robotic systems are more efficient than their conventional counterparts.

Precise navigation of the bot is extremely important in between the rows of the crop because any small error can damage the plants. This issue is resolved in another study[11] by proposing a non-linear model predictive approach. The controller algorithm is verified through simulations and has been examined under several path constraints. Aspects like convergence time and precision are tested, and the proposed controller is said to perform better. A low-cost and environment-friendly bot was designed in a study [12]. It eliminated the use of chemicals for the removal of weeds. The robotic arm of the bot has a high-speed blade that chops off the weed. The classification of weeds and crops is performed by using the RCNN algorithm. An in-depth survey of different techniques used for the autonomous removal of weeds is elaborated in a study [13]. It mainly describes technologies used for guiding the bot, identification of weed, precision in terms of killing the weed, and positioning and movement of the bot. Some of the several challenges reported in the paper include occlusion, bad weather conditions, and robustness of the segmentation and weed detection methods, which limit its commercial usage. There is still scope for developing a fully optimized system that is commercially deployable.

Weed removal bot is used to generate a dataset of soybean fields in different environments with the help of multiple sensors such as a camera, GPS system, inertial measurement unit, and odometry [14]. A system with multiple cameras is designed with computer vision, CNN and tracking algorithms to provide high precision in weed removal [15]. It is tested for in-row removal of weeds through simulation and in fields and is evaluated in terms of detection, classification, tracking and actuation accuracy.

Global navigation satellite system, compass and camera were used in a study [16] to accurately navigate the bot in rice fields. Considerably good accuracy was achieved in guiding the bot through the rows of crops, and the bot was put to the test in many circumstances like large number of weeds, plants, and bad weather. The accuracy reduces when there are a large number of weeds.

A systemic review is presented in another study [17], which compares and analyzes different techniques used for weed detection. Deep learning techniques such as CNN, IoT, data analytics, drones, and computer vision provide superior performance in precision agriculture. The main challenges in weed detection include the robustness of the system to lighting and environmental conditions, scalability, heterogeneity in the shape and size of crops, interoperability in technologies, etc. YOLOX CNN is used to discriminate weed and corn sapling, monocular ranging is used by the bot to accurately locate the weed, and a blue light laser is used to kill the weed [18]. Object detection and segmentation of crops and weeds are performed using deep learning [19]. Several segmentation models are compared in terms of training time, precision and confusion matrix.

SYSTEM FOR AUTONOMOUS ERADICATION OF WEEDS

The development of agriculture, its productivity, and its factors are of global importance. Weeds are almost certainly unavoidable because of recent climatic changes. Therefore, weed detection technology is necessary. These weeds may be brought on by birds, insects, previous crops, etc. These weeds deprive the soil of nutrients while also obstructing sunshine, water, and space and possibly causing damage. The loss or harm that a plant cannot bear results in the plant's demise. There is a push for this weed-detecting technology as a result of these unresolved issues.

Not just weed detection but also the weed-killing process needs a major upgrade. With the conventional weed-killing process, the major flaw is that it is not efficient as all the non-effected crops will also get sprayed by weed killer, which causes wastage of spray and lowers the food quality with increased toxic contents, as it can harm the crops.

This necessitates continual observation of the leaf's texture, appearance, and other traits. Manpower-based weed detection of the plant is more labor-intensive and dangerous since workers must adhere to regulations for body covering; also, the detection may not be as precise and clear as it should be, resulting in the usage or application of incorrect pesticides or weed killers. However, accuracy and risk are reduced when machine identification of disease is combined with autonomous weed-type detection.

A pesticide sprayer motor is carried by a bot. The bot goes over the field using an ultrasonic sensor and stops when it senses a plant stem identically to the ultrasonic sensor positioned above the robot's base. When the robot stops, the leaves are photographed by the Pi camera. To determine the precise leaf disease condition, many techniques are applied to the collected image. The block diagram of the automatic weed controller is depicted in Fig. (1).

Fig. (1))
Block diagram of the weed control system with smart automation.

Pre-processing: The goal of this is to enhance the image data, which lessens undesirable distortion and feature-related images for improved future processing. Then, it takes in and acquires an image as input and outputs a slightly altered version of that image.

Operations like image denoising, segmentation, and content image enhancement are all considered to be part of preprocessing. Measurements, either geometric or segmented and in pertinent image regions, are what feature extraction is defined to do. A collection of integers that reflect certain plant characteristics or other plant parts identified through image classification is used to define a function. All of the collected elements are mutually coordinated into an element vector at this stage of classification, which makes it an alternative classification.

For every image, we employ a Neural Network (NN). After dividing the image into areas, the NN calculates the probability and bounding box for each area. Probability estimations are used to determine the weights of these boxes. During the testing process, every image is examined, and classification is carried out. Unlike R-CNN, which requires thousands of evaluations of an image, it also generates predictions from a single evaluation network. Compared to R-CNN, it is faster.

In digital image processing, three types of images are typically employed: Binary images, grayscale images, and color images. Because each pixel in a binary image is represented as a single 0 and 1 bit, binary images are also known as two-level images. Grayscale images are a type of digital black and white image that is made up of shades of grey (0, 255), as opposed to black (0), which is the color with the weakest intensity, and white, which is the color with the maximum intensity (255). A color picture contains color data. The degree of color breakdown is expressed as a number between 0 and 255. Red, green, and blue (R, G, B) are the three primary colors represented by this

Algorithm: For computer vision, machine learning, and image processing, there is a sizable open-source library called Open CV. Python, C++, Java, and many other programming languages are supported by OpenCV. It can analyze pictures and movies to find faces, objects, and even human handwriting. The number of weapons in your arsenal expands when it is integrated with different libraries, such as the highly optimized library for numerical operations known as NumPy. Open-CV may then be used to combine any operations that NumPy can perform.

We can detect the disease reasonably accurately with the aid of multiple template matching, an algorithm written over the template matching function in the Python Open-CV library. Once the disease is identified, a microcontroller and actuators are used to select the necessary fertilizer and spray it on the required area and in the required amount, which is per-calculated by programming and calibrating the device. The project is carried out on the identification of weeds in the field of sesame crops. In Figs. (2 and 3), the detection of sesame crops and weeds is indicated.

Fig. (2))
Detection of sesame crop.
Fig. (3))
Detection of weeds.

After the camera's record function is initiated, the camera's lens focuses light through the camera aperture and light filters onto the electronic image sensor. The image sensor is arranged in a grid pattern, with each individual square called a pixel. The image sensor cannot determine the color of the light recorded; it only detects the intensity. Color is typically identified by the use of a color filter, which allows one color of light from the visible spectrum into each pixel. The color filter is usually arranged in a Bayer filter pattern, which is used to average the colors of a 2 x 2 pixel square.

Since the filter produces some inaccuracy, any discoloring is called interpolation. A different technique for identifying colors makes use of various image sensors, each of which is focused on capturing a specific aspect of the color image, such as one color. The output is then combined to create the whole color image. Instead of having inbuilt filters on the sensors, they often use color separation devices like beam splitters. The hardware requirements for making the bot are pan-tilt servo, water pump, Raspberry Pi 4 Model-B, and camera module. The prototype of the weed killer bot is shown in Fig. (4).

The automatic weed remover robot demonstrated successful performance in the task of weed detection and removal, as validated through extensive testing and evaluation. The results highlight the effectiveness of the robot in accurately identifying and selectively removing weeds while minimizing damage to the surrounding vegetation.

Fig. (4))
(a) Prototype of robot for detecting weed (b) Camera module (c) Circuit with Raspberry Pi (d) Samples of crop and weed.

Weed Detection Accuracy: The robot's computer vision algorithms achieved a high level of accuracy in weed detection. Through the analysis of visual data captured by onboard cameras, the robot successfully distinguished between weeds and desired plants with an average detection accuracy of over 90%. This reliable detection capability ensures precise targeting of weeds during the removal process.

Weed Removal Efficiency: The robot exhibited efficient weed removal capabilities, significantly reducing the presence of weeds in the target area. The implementation of the automatic weed remover robot resulted in significant time and cost savings compared to traditional manual weeding methods. Autonomous operation reduced the labor-intensive nature of weeding, allowing farmers and gardeners to allocate their resources more efficiently. The reduced reliance on manual labor and herbicides also contributed to cost savings and a more environmentally friendly approach to weed control.

Overall, the results obtained from the automatic weed remover robot showcased its effectiveness, efficiency, and adaptability in weed management. The successful performance in weed detection, selective removal, and operational autonomy demonstrates the potential of this technology to revolutionize weed control practices in agriculture and gardening, offering improved efficiency, reduced labor, and enhanced environmental sustainability.

CONCLUSION

The development and implementation of an automatic weed remover robot have shown great promise in addressing the challenges faced in weed management. The robot has been designed to autonomously detect and remove weeds, offering a potential solution to the labor-intensive and time-consuming task of manual weeding in various agricultural and gardening settings. An efficient system is designed to integrate machine learning techniques and advanced computer vision algorithms and to accurately identify and classify weeds from the surrounding vegetation. This allows for precise targeting and selective removal of unwanted plants while minimizing damage to the desired crops or plants. Moreover, the robot's ability to operate autonomously enables it to cover large areas and operate for extended periods without human intervention. This scalability and autonomy make it suitable for a wide range of applications, from small-scale gardens to large agricultural fields.

While the automatic weed remover robot has shown promising results, there are still areas for further improvement and optimization. Enhancing the robot's navigation capabilities to handle various terrains and weather conditions, as well as expanding its weed detection capabilities to handle different species, are avenues worth exploring. Additionally, incorporating intelligent decision-making algorithms that can adapt to varying weed densities and growth patterns can enhance the overall efficiency of the system. Overall, the automatic weed remover robot presents a transformative solution to weed management, offering increased efficiency, reduced reliance on manual labor, and environmentally friendly weed control methods. Continued research, development, and real-world testing will undoubtedly lead to further advancements and wider adoption of this technology, ultimately revolutionizing the way to approach weed control in agriculture.

Origin and History of AI, IoT and Blockchain Technology and their Pertinence in Food Supply Chain Management



Sudhanand Prasad Lal1, *, Abhinab Borah1, Sakshi Pundir1, Leezamoni Das1, Diksha Srivastava1
1 Dr. Rajendra Prasad Central Agricultural University, Pusa(Bihar), India

Abstract

In a world where hunger and malnourishment are still a matter of concern, food loss, wastage, and its timely availability must be addressed immediately. Based on an extensive literature review and secondary data, this manuscript has tried to come up with holistic, contemporary solutions for food supply chain management. Against this backdrop, tools viz., IoT, AI, and blockchain will not only manage food loss, wastage, and timely availability but also revamp transparency, food quality and safety. IoT architecture offers tracking solutions through measurement, network, service, and application layers using various sensors and WiFi-connected devices. Artificial Intelligence (AI) and the Internet of Things are becoming significant facilitators of supply chain management (SCM) optimization. The adoption of AI technology has been associated with a 15% reduction in logistics costs, a 35% reduction in inventory levels, and a 65% increase in customer service levels. It assists in demand forecasting, enhanced safety, cost saving, boosting revenue, and chiefly on-time delivery. Block Chain Technology (BCT) system can be programmed to record and track food supply chain transactions. Chhattisgarh in India has developed the Centralized Online Real-Time Electronic Public Distribution System (CORE-PDS) and has become a model state in the ‘Public Distribution System (PDS)’. Unfortunately, Chhattisgarh’s model fell prey to scams viz., bogus ration cards, irregularities in rice stock, and poor quality rice and salt samples collected, which were unsuitable for human consumption because of no mid-term audit and monitoring. The chapter concludes that all these anomalies would not have happened to such an extent or have been addressed timely if the technological trinity viz., IoT, AI, and Blockchain, had been incorporated judiciously into the PDS system.

Keywords: Blockchain, Chhattisgarh’s PDS Model, Food Supply Chain, Internet of Autonomous Things (IoAT), Radio Frequency Identification (RFID), Wireless Sensor Networks (WSNs).

* Corresponding author Sudhanand Prasad Lal: Dr. Rajendra Prasad Central Agricultural University, Pusa(Bihar), India; Tel: 7876890810; E-mail: sudhanand.lal@rpcau.ac.in

INTRODUCTION

In April 2023, India surpassed China as the world's most populous country. On the contrary, in the 2022 Global Hunger Index, India fell under serious categories and was ranked 107th out of 121 countries, having a score of 29.1. The irony is that India's estimated economic value of post-harvest losses of just 45 crops was Rs. 92651 crores at average annual prices of 2014. AI, IoT and BCT are ubiquitous in the present world, but conceptual clarity among all these tools is rare. AI is the expression of an artificial actuality meant for perceiving, synthesizing, and inferring information. Economic, medical, engineering, and military departments frequently use artificial intelligence systems, in addition to many common home computer software operations, strategy games, and other video games. The term AI refers to both the intelligence of machines and the area of computer science that aims to develop it. The word then officially devised by Marvin Minsky and John McCarthy in the year 1956 (Table 1), who hosted a research project for 8 weeks called Dartmouth Summer Research Project on Artificial Intelligence (DSRPAI)in the United States.

Table 1 Fathers/Pioneers of AI, IoT, BCT and ChatGPT.
S. No. Particulars Year Father/Pioneer Source
1. AI 1955 John McCarthy Encyclopedia Britannica (2023) [1]
2. Godfather of AI 2012 Geoffrey Hinton Taylor & Hern (2023) [2]

AI concentrates on the advancements of intelligent machines that are proficient in performing tasks that require the use of human intellect. By developing algorithms and models, computers and systems are able to simulate cognitive functions such as learning, reasoning, problem solving and making decisions. As a result of artificial intelligence, large amounts of data can be analyzed, patterns can be recognized, predictions can be made, and actions can be taken based on the information gained. Advances in artificial intelligence, such as improved algorithms, autonomous vehicles, virtual assistants, fraud detection systems, personalized medicine and smart homes, have been developed. Besides, recent years have seen a significant increase in attention and popularity for Blockchain Technology (BCT). Using this system, multiple parties can record and transact securely and transparently across multiple ledgers without an intermediary being necessary. By creating the first cryptocurrency, Bitcoin, Satoshi Nakamoto introduced the concept of Blockchain in 2008. It is important to note that at its core, a Blockchain is a chain of blocks containing a list of transactions within each block. A chain of immutable and tamper-proof blocks is formed by linking these blocks using cryptographic hashes. A Blockchain is a decentralized network that cannot be controlled by any single entity, which makes it resistant to censorship and manipulation. While the Internet of Things (IoT) denotes a network of interconnected devices, such as machines, appliances, and cars, which can collect and exchange data through the use of sensors, software, and network connectivity (Table 1). By enabling these devices to communicate and interact with one another and with humans, IoT creates an intelligent and seamless ecosystem. Sensors in IoT devices are capable of capturing data such as temperature, humidity, light, motion, and location. As a result of the transmission of this information through networks, either locally or via the Internet, the data is then stored, analyzed, and further processed on centralized servers or cloud-based platforms.

In Table 2, varied widely accepted definitions of artificial intelligence (AI) have been compiled. As these definitions are from the pioneer workers, they are consequently widely cited.

Table 2 Definition of Artificial Intelligence (AI).
S. No. Definition Given by Source
1 “The science and engineering of making intelligent machines”. McCarthy, 1956 (Dartmouth, 1956) [6]
2 “Capability of machines to communicate with and imitate the capabilities of humans”. Schutzer, 1990 (Schutzer, 1990) [7]
3 “A program which, in an arbitrary world, will cope not worse than a human”. Dobrev, 2004 (Dobrev, 2004) [8]
4 “A system’s ability to interpret external data correctly, to learn from such data, and to use those learnings to achieve specific goals and tasks through flexible adaptation”. Haenlein and Kaplan, 2019 (Haenlein & Kaplan, 2019) [9]

The phrase “4 seasons of AI” has a widely recognized or standardized meaning in the field of artificial intelligence (AI). It refers to a specific framework, concept, or model that was introduced in 1942 (Table 3). AI is a rapidly evolving field, and new frameworks and ideas are constantly emerging.

Table 3 Timeline of Artificial Intelligence encompassing the Past: 4 seasons of AI.
The origin of AI
1942 Issac Asimov publishes scientification story collection “I, Robot.”
1950 The English mathematician Alan Turing launches the Turing Test (computer's ability to “think” is determined by this test) in his manuscript “Computing Machinery and Intelligence”.
1956 A conference at Dartmouth coins the term “Artificial Intelligence”(Computer scientist Nathaniel Rochester created the first scientific computer i.e. IBM -701)
The Rise and Fall of AI
1964-66 ELIZA computer program was created by Joseph Weizenbaum at MIT.
1968 HAL 9000 is the sentient and deadly computer featured in Clarke and Kubrick’s film “2001: A Space Odyssey”
1974-early 1980s The first Winter of AI, a span when funding was decreased, and attention in the field waned as boost became disillusionment.
1978 In the science fiction television series “Battlestar Galactica”, warrior robots called Cylons were introduced.
1984 An artificial intelligence called Skynet controls the killing machines in the first “Terminator” film.
1985 McKinion and Lemmon introduced the application of AI in agriculture with GOSSYM (cotton crop simulation model utilizing expert systems)
Sept. 28, 1987 “Star Trek: The Next Generation” commenced Lieutenant Commander Data i.e., self-aware android.
1987-93 The second Winter of AI.
AI Fall: The Harvest
May 11, 1997 World chess winner Garry Kasparov was overpowered by IBM's Deep Blue computer.

2005 Stanford vehicle: won the DARPA grand challenge

Oct 14, 2011 The iPhone 4S comes with Apple's intelligent personal aide Siri.
June, 2012 The Google Brain computer cluster learns to identify cats from YouTube videos based on millions of images.
August, 2014 It was decided at the 2015 workshop that researchers should develop a new Turing test.
2015 A Google program called AlphaGo proved successful in defeating the world defender in the board game Go.
Source: (Tate, 2014) [10]; (Haenlein & Kaplan, 2019) [9].

METHODOLOGY

Based on an extensive literature review and secondary data, this manuscript has tried to come up with holistic, contemporary solutions for food supply chain management. The study was further strengthened through gap analysis and needs assessment of the present study.

Evolution and History of IoT

1. “Human to Human”: Pre-internet technology (e.g., fixed and mobile phone, SMS)

2. “WWW”: Internet of Content (e.g., e-mail, information, entertainment)

3. “WEB 2.0”: Internet of Services (e.g., e-productivity, e-commerce)

5. Internet of Things: “Machine to Machine” e.g., identification, tracking, monitoring, metering [11]. The milestones of IoT are described in detail inTable 4.

Table 4 Milestones of IoT.
S. No. Year Specifications/Descriptions
1. 1832 Baron Schilling invented an electromagnetic telegraph in Russia. Carl Friedrich Gauss and Wilhelm Weber created their own code in 1832 to communicate across a 1200 m distance in Gottingen, Germany.
2. 1892 Nikola Tesla developed the first basic radio design, and on November 8, 1898, he received a patent for a wireless radio-controlled robot boat, the first of its kind.
3. 1974 Transmission Control Protocol/Internet Protocol (TCP/IP)
4. 1982 The Coca-Cola vending machine that was run by Carnegie Mellon University in Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania programs, was the first connected gadget to be created.
5. 1990 John Romkey introduced the TCP/IP protocol, allowing toast (collective networks) to be connected to the internet.
6. 1995 Siemens released the first cellular module built by M2M (Machine to Machine).
7. 1998 The release of Internet Protocol Version 6 (IPV6).
8. 1999 Kevin Ashton coined the phrase ‘IoT’ while working for P&G (Procter and Gamble). Kevin made an internet and RFID connection (Radio-Frequency Identification).
9. 2000 LG introduced the first internet-enabled refrigerator.
10. 2003-2004 Major media outlets like The Guardian, Scientific American, and the Boston Globe used the term “Internet of Things” in their articles. The word IoT started to emerge in book titles for the first time, with initiatives such as Cooltown, Internet0,and Disappearing Computer initiative, aimed to put ideas into practice.
11. 2005 Significant advancements marked for the Internet of Things according to the first report of The International Telecommunication Union (ITU) of the UN.
12. 2008 The EU recognized the IoT; consequently, the First European IoT Conference took place.
13. 2011 Google Nest self-learning Wi-Fi thermostat was released. Large-scale teaching and marketing campaigns on the subject were initiated by Cisco, IBM, and Ericsson. The IoT is now available to DIYers (Do It Yourself) who are interested in the topic because of the maturation of the Arduino (acts as the brain of the system and processes the data from sensors; it is an open-source hardware platform) and other hardware platforms.
14. 2011-2017 Low-power chipsets with integrated 3G/4G connectivity got smaller, more potent, and more affordable to make. IoT is being used by a growing number of businesses and homeowners.
Source [12].

Applications of IoT

• Can help in food transportation procurement using RFID technology. Among the IoT tools to track food products, RFID technology is the most effective and expensive [13].

• Can help in the production and improvement of food safety

• Helps in providing facilitation to food manufactures with accurate prediction of future issues.

• Helps in maintaining the integrity of perishable food if worked smartly, as some products require special preservation.

• Helps in improving logistics.

• Helps in food authenticity by providing digital identification marks (digital tags) on any given product.

• Helps in monitoring mechanical damage in any machine, which directly helps in the quality of food.

• Helps in demand prediction according to the preference of consumer.

• Helps in locating goods in the warehouse, which in turn improves staff safety.

• Helps in monitoring storage conditions through environmental sensors, which can make changes accordingly in shipping circumstances.

• Reduces dependency on human data entry or conventional inventory devices.

IoT in the Food Supply Chain

Nowadays, there are large numbers of top trending technologies for various day-to-day activities. The Internet of Things is also among these technologies, which has been playing its role in various activities. Since India’s large population depends on agriculture for its survival, we need to find the role of this technology in the food supply chain [14].

IoT is rapidly transforming the food supply, improving transparency and ensuring the safety of the food that we eat. It offers various benefits that can help in ensuring the quality and safety of food products throughout the supply chain. The major benefit of IoT is improved transparency. It has made it easy for companies to trace the source of food and monitor its progress through the supply chain by enabling tracking solutions that help in identifying problems and issues with food supply at any stage. Various sensors and devices can help monitor food quality, temperature, and other environmental factors, ensuring food safety requirements.

This technology can also help consumers by giving detailed information about the food they buy since companies are able to trace the source of the food. It can affect how everything works, including how food is produced, processed, stored, distributed, and consumed on farms.

The architecture of IoT in the food supply chain

• Measurement layer data deals with time, location, temperature, machine, and other information.

• Network layer gathers data at the sensor layer and communicates it to the service layer by means of Wireless Sensor Networks (WSNs), Bluetooth, and Wi-Fi routers.

• Service layer contains a variety of analytics engines/tools and services where data is stored/kept.

• Application layer is made up of several modules and programs (e.g., production effectiveness, food quality).

Widely accepted definitions of Blockchain

1) “Blockchain is a shared, immutable ledger that facilitates the process of recording transactions and tracking assets in a business network. An asset can be tangible (a house, car, cash and land) or intangible (intellectual property, patents, copyrights, branding). Virtually anything of value can be tracked and traded on a Blockchain network, reducing risk and cutting costs for all involved” [15].

2) Blockchain technology is a decentralized, distributed ledger [16].

3) Blockchain is a secure database shared across a network of participants [17].

From the above definitions, it can be concluded that Blockchain is:

1) A Shared Platform- The chain is shared among the network of members involved.

2) De-centralized and Distributed ledger- A digital ledger that can be viewed by a permitted audience, but no one person has absolute authority over the data.

3) Immutable- No data can be deleted or modified without the concern (acceptance) of at least 51% of the members in the network.

4) Secure- Since the ledger is distributed over a very large number of computers, it is impossible for a hacker to hack every single computer connected to the network.

Looking back at Blockchain

Cryptographically secured transactions have been the basis of the Blockchain concept since 1991 when Stuart Haber and Wakefield Scott Stornetta introduced it. There was, however, not much development until 2008. In 2008, Satoshi Nakamoto (a pseudonym) created the first child of this technology, which gained traction and widespread usage. The first cryptocurrency using BCT, named Bitcoin, was launched in 2009, paving the way for Blockchain's impact in the tech world (Table 5).

Table 5 Timeline of milestone events in Blockchain development.
S. No. Milestone Events Year
1 Stuart Haber and Wakefield Scott Stornetta proposed a cryptographically safe chain of records called blocks. 1991
2 Satoshi Nakamoto devised the technology's concept and anticipated applications, and the first Blockchain and cryptocurrency were formally introduced. 2008
3 IBM revealed a Blockchain plan for cloud-based business solutions 2017
4 The Japanese government acknowledged the legitimacy of Blockchain and
cryptocurrency.		 2018
5 Dubai declared that its government would be powered by Blockchain by 2020. 2019
6 Facebook promised to launch its BCT 2020

Characteristics of Blockchain

1) Digital assets are disseminated rather than copied or transmitted.

2) Digital assets can be accessed and governed and are transparent in real-time by several parties due to their distributed and decentralized nature.

3) All changes to the Blockchain ledger are recorded, maintaining the integrity and security of the distributed ledger.

4) Due to the fact that Blockchain ledgers are public and are built with inherent security safeguards, they are an ideal solution for practically any sector.

Application of Blockchain in Agriculture

1) Transparent transaction management

2) Efficient financing

3) Improved financial support for developing countries

4) Efficient smart farm contracts

5) Agriculture Insurance: Safety net for farmers

6) Land registries

7) Monitoring data

Blockchain for Food Supply Chain Management

A food supply chain is a complicated system of activities and procedures that include multiple phases, such as food product manufacturing, processing, packaging, distribution, and consumption. It encompasses all of the actions involved in the transfer of food items from the farm to the end user's plate.

Trying to control the food supply chain can provide a slew of challenges. Food safety, quality control, supply chain interruptions, and environmental sustainability may be among the issues. As a result, there is an increasing need for BCT to increase transparency, traceability, and efficiency in the food supply chain.

Blockchain

The use of BCT may be useful in managing food supply chains by facilitating a transparent and secure system for tracking the movement of food products from their origin to their destination. Here are some ways in which Blockchain can be used to manage food supply chains:

1) Traceability: As a result of the use of the Blockchain, it is possible to track food items from the farmer to the consumer in a tamper-proof manner by keeping a record of all the transactions that occur in the supply chain. When a food safety issue arises, this information can also be utilized to identify the source of contamination or spoilage.

2) Transparency: Blockchain is a distributed ledger, which means the database is shared among all the stakeholders. This ensures utmost transparency and smooth communication between stakeholders and makes management easy.

3) Quality control: A definite quality standard can be set, and a digital record of the quality of products can be maintained at various stages of production, storage, and transportation. This ensures that only those food items that match the previous set quality standard reach the end consumer.

4) Efficiency: Blockchain can also help in automating inventory management, order tracking, and financial transactions to save costs and improve efficiency.

5) Sustainability: Blockchain can help reduce food wastage by creating a system of tracking, conserving resources, and promoting sustainable production practices.

Blockchain technology can provide a powerful tool for managing food supply chains and improving efficiency, transparency, and accountability across the entire supply chain.

Any Food Supply Chain Starts with the following:

1) The producer (farmer): The information that can be recorded during this stage is altitude, irrigation treatment, temperature, humidity, soil fertility, date of harvesting, and dispatching to the next link in the chain. Legal certificates like Organic Farming Certificates (if any) are also recorded at this stage.

2) Food processors: Information that is recorded at this stage may include location, receiving date, processes, storage and packaging details, shelf life, temperature, date of dispatch, etc.

3) The distributor: Information that is recorded at this stage is again similar to the previous link, like location, receiving date, inventory details, storage condition, information of retailers and wholesalers, and finally, dispatched date to the next link.

4) Retailer: The information recorded in this stage are location, receiving date, and sales information. When the product is sold to the end consumer, the sale information is also stored so that no other sale on the same label can be made.

5) The end consumer: They are the final link in the food supply chain; thus, they have the full right to know every detail about the food they are consuming. This is possible with the help of Blockchain, where the authenticity, origin, and journey can be visible to the end consumer.

Genesis and History of Public Distribution System (PDS)

Food shortages during World War II led to the conception of the PDS in 1942 in India, where it evolved into an ultramodern program (Fig. 2). As a part of the British government's effort to ensure equitable distribution of food to civic consumers after high prices in 1939, rationing was instituted by Bombay in 1939 [18]. Consequently, the policy of stabilizing and operating food grains has its roots in the practice of rationing during times of extreme hardship, particularly during times of shortage. The PDS was designed to ensure that essential supplies are readily accessible to consumers everywhere in the country. In this system, foodgrains, sugar, and other necessities such as kerosene oil and edible oil are made available to the citizens of the state at fair prices in order to meet their basic needs. As a way to keep inflationary tendencies under control, a close monitoring system is necessary to make PDS an effective tool against numerous influences on the open market [18]. Targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS) was introduced by the Indian government in June 1997 as a means of providing assistance to the underprivileged. It was mandated that states develop and implement plans to identify the poor in order to distribute food grains and to distribute those grains at the Fair Price Shop (FPS) level in an ethical and responsible manner (National Food Security Portal, 2023) [19].

Evolution of PDS in India

Fig. (2))
Timeline of PDS in India.

Public Distribution System (PDS) in India

A key component of the government's program for managing the nation's food economy, the PDS evolved as a mechanism for managing food shortages by distributing foodgrains at a low cost (Fig. 3). Rice, wheat, sugar, and kerosene are distributed to states and UTs for distribution under the PDS [20].

Fig. (3))
Importance of PDS [20].

What is the current state of using modern ICT in the food sector?

Food security is provided to 81.35 crore citizens each month through the Targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS) (Table 6). This program is regulated by the National Food Security Act (NFSA, 2013). In order to modernize and reform the public distribution system through technology, the government is currently implementing the SMART-PDS scheme. A great deal of data will be generated in this project that can be used to enhance the delivery of other government welfare programs and schemes. According to the Food Ministry, the SMART-PDS program is expected to cost approximately 350 crores, which will be divided among the states and implemented over a 3 years period between 2023 and 2025.

Table 6 Key challenges in the PDS supply chain.
Problems Cause Effect References
Movement of food grain Concentration of procurement activities in a small number of states despite PDS's nationwide operations Costly logistics for shipping, a delay in delivery, and insufficient control over stock monitoring Banerjee (2011) [21]
Excess cost Low monitoring and control;
high administrative
cost		 Higher government subsidy burden Ahluwalia (1993); Jha & Ramaswami (2010) [22]
Concentration in certain crops Price stabilization mechanism developed through Minimum Support Price (MSP) Reserved commodity surpluses reduce the availability of other commodities. Ramaswami (2002) [23]
Speculation in the market High procurement and later selling them on the open market Decreases private stocks, which raises consumer prices on the open market and creates uncertainty about how to manage the demand-supply imbalance. Ramaswami (2002) [23]

What is SMART-PDS?

In order to improve the efficiency, transparency, and accountability of the country’s targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS), the Indian government launched the SMART-PDS (Scheme for Modernization and Reform through Technology in Public Distribution System), as illustrated in (Fig. 4) [24].

Fig. (4))
Objectives of the SMART-PDS initiative.

“Food for all Model” of Chhattisgarh

In Chhattisgarh, PDS coverage is universal as a result of the coexistence of the National Food Security Act, 2003 (NFSA) and the Chhattisgarh Food Security Act, 2012 [25]. An innovation in Chhattisgarh is the implementation of an ICT-enabled Universal Public Distribution System (UPDS) that is designed to ensure “Food For All” and ensure a variety of dimensions of food security, including accessibility, utilization, stability, and agency. As a result of the political context, Chhattisgarh's food security programs have been designed with practical and applicable technological solutions.

• Through the deployment of the ICT-driven Centralized Online Real-time Electronic CORE-PDS in 2011-2012, the UPDS has become accessible and movable to the state's beneficiaries. As a general rule, the CORE-PDS is an online distribution system, but there will be some limited offline deals in the event of temporary connectivity issues at Fair Price Shops.

• By authenticating themselves through any of the multiple instruments permitted, beneficiaries in the CORE-PDS can access their portions from any state that has notified FPS. It is interesting to know that the technological architecture of the RSBY scheme is designed in such a manner that little effort is required for its expansion to other social security schemes as it is prepared to include the necessary information for benefit delivery to other schemes [26] i.e., Smart Ration Card/PDS. In addition, the robust technology platform of RSBY has contributed to standardizing operations among all stakeholders.

• As the FPS salesperson inserts the smart card in the electronic Point of Sales (ePoS) device, the number on the smart card is read and transmitted to the server via General Packet Radio Services (GPRS) for the purpose of obtaining beneficiary entitlement balances.

• Upon receiving the beneficiary's amount, the FPS salesman enters the details of the sale and submits them to the system. An update to the sale is made by the server, which generates a success report. The beneficiaries receive a receipt in addition to the goods or food grains.

Centralized Online Real-Time Electronic Public Distribution System (CORE-PDS) of Chhattisgarh in India

A major objective of the Indian Government is to eliminate black marketing and improve distribution efficiency through the computerization of more than 50% of fair shops by March 2017 [27]. In 2007, Chhattisgarh was the first state to computerize the PDS supply chain, and in 2012, it introduced the Centralized Online Real-Time Electronic Public Distribution System (CORE-PDS), which has become the model for other states. A forced monopoly was established by the owners of the fair price shops when each beneficiary household was tied to a particular fair price shop. Because fair price shops competed with one another for customers by offering better services in order to avoid losing them, every beneficiary could select the fair price shop from which s/he would purchase their rations. ICT was employed to create an effective and transparent system. Thus, all fair price shops have Point of Sale (POS) systems with internet access, and smart cards are provided to recipients. A POS device is used to swipe a smart card to verify eligibility after the salesperson inputs the desired quantity of commodities, which is updated with the central server. A gadget generates a receipt that is presented to the beneficiary after the payment is accepted and the beneficiary receives the commodity (Fig. 5).

Fig. (5))
Cyclic pentagonal model of chhattisgarh PDS system.

Phases of Chhattisgarh’s Model

It became a model state in the Public Distribution System and was also praised by the union government and Supreme Court, which ordered other states to follow its model [28]. The state was able to do all this in 3 distinct phases:

• First phase: The state government brought a number of changes to PDS delivery and procurement by eliminating the middlemen, de-privatizing all ration shops, ensuring the establishment of ration shops in each gram panchayat, and providing ration to cardholders.

• Second phase: To bring more transparency, efforts were increased, the public was involved, and the system was made online. For this, call centres were established, and all the information was available on the website regarding the collection and distribution of rations.

• Third phase: The procurement process of paddy was strengthened, and an online system was also introduced for this. Also, arrangements were made to transfer payments to farmers’ bank accounts directly.

Conclusion and Future Perspectives

With the use of internet technology, the food supply chain is able to use dynamic permutation in food management operations. The use of these techniques can help in making the food supply chain a more transparent system, which will help the government as well as the consumers. This can help in the collection of real-time data, and analyzing this data becomes easier, which automatically leads to an improved food supply chain. We need to educate people more about these technologies for the full use of such things. Today, AI, IoT and BCT are conspicuous everywhere, from economics to medical science and from engineering to Agriculture. IoT uses RFID technology, which tracks product locations throughout the entire supply chain, as it eliminates stock-outs in the supply chain. In BCT, data of transactions is stored in blocks with the hash (encrypted secured information) of that block and the hash of the previous block. Any attempt to change the data will result in the changing of the hash number, which will automatically be denied due to a mismatch with the hash, which makes it tamper-proof. Chhattisgarh’s PDS model has become a model state in the Public Distribution System and was also praised by the union government and Supreme Court, which ordered other states to follow its model. But there are specific shortcomings of the Chhattisgarh model, which may be addressed by the incorporation of AI, IoT and BCT. Furthermore, for truly implementing ONORC (One Nation One Ration Card) without flaw, the application of AI, IoT and BCT is quintessential.
