Food Supply Chain Management by Leveraging AI, IoT, and Blockchain Technologies



K. Radhika1, *, Sheena Mohammed1, Satya Kiranmai Tadepalli1, Kiranmaie Puvvula1
1 Chaitanya Bharathi Institute of Technology, Hyderabad, India

Abstract

Traceability is an important component of food supply chain management because it allows businesses to track food products from their origin to the point of consumption. However, the current food supply chain system frequently lacks proper traceability methods, making it difficult to pinpoint the source of food safety concerns, monitor product quality, and assure regulatory compliance. As a result, there is a growing need to rethink food supply chain management to assure traceability. Artificial intelligence (AI), the Internet of Things (IoT), and blockchain are contributing to the resolution of these issues by increasing accountability, traceability, and efficiency. The application of blockchain, IoT, and AI to food supply chain management is examined in this book chapter. It highlights the possible advantages of incorporating these technologies into the food industry, including improved food safety, real-time food quality monitoring, and increased supply chain visibility. It looks at other uses as well, such as decentralized food traceability systems, smart packaging, and predictive analytics. The chapter also discusses the obstacles and restrictions that come with implementing these technologies in the food supply chain, as well as potential solutions and effectiveness.

Keywords: Consumer trust, Donsumption, Data analytics, Distribution, Efficiency, Food safety risks, Inventory management, Market dynamics, Quality control, Real-time monitoring, Regulatory compliance, Traceability, Transparency, Transportation, Visibility.

* Corresponding author K. Radhika: Chaitanya Bharathi Institute of Technology, Hyderabad, India; Tel: 9346253642; E-mail: kradhika_aids@cbit.ac.in

INTRODUCTION

Food supply chain management [1] refers to the process of overseeing and managing the flow of food products from the point of origin to the consumer. It encompasses all the activities involved in the production, processing, storage, transportation, and distribution of food items [2]. The food supply chain shown in Fig. (1) consists of several interconnected stages.

Fig. (1))
Supply chain management.

Procurement: This involves sourcing raw materials, such as agricultural produce, ingredients, and packaging materials, from suppliers, farmers, or other sources.

Processing: Raw materials are transformed into finished food products through various manufacturing and processing operations. This stage may include cleaning, sorting, cutting, cooking, packaging, and labeling.

Storage: Processed food products are stored in warehouses or distribution centers before they are transported to retailers or consumers. Proper storage conditions, such as temperature control, are crucial to maintain product quality and safety.

Transportation: Food products are moved from storage facilities to distribution centers, retailers, or directly to consumers. This stage requires efficient logistics management, including route optimization, vehicle selection, and compliance with food safety and transportation regulations.

Distribution: Food products are distributed to various points of sale, such as grocery stores, restaurants, and institutions. This may involve working with wholesalers, distributors, or direct delivery to retailers. Inventory management, order fulfillment, and demand forecasting are essential aspects of distribution.

Retail: Food products are made available to consumers through retail outlets, both physical stores and online platforms. Retailers play a significant role in product placement, pricing, marketing, and ensuring a positive consumer experience.

Consumption: The final stage occurs when consumers purchase and consume the food products. Consumer feedback and preferences play a crucial role in shaping the supply chain and product development.

Food supply chain management involves coordinating and managing activities across all these stages to ensure the efficient and timely delivery of safe and quality food products [3]. It requires collaboration and information sharing among various stakeholders, including suppliers, manufacturers, distributors, retailers, and consumers.

Effective food supply chain management is crucial for meeting the demands of a growing population, ensuring food security, minimizing waste, and promoting sustainability. It involves addressing challenges such as food safety risks, quality control, inventory management, traceability, regulatory compliance, and responding to market dynamics.

Advancements in technology, such as AI, IoT, and blockchain, have the potential to optimize and streamline food supply chain management by improving visibility, traceability, efficiency, and transparency [4]. These technologies enable real-time monitoring, data analytics, automation, and secure data sharing, leading to enhanced operational effectiveness and consumer trust [5].

ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE IN FOOD SUPPLY CHAIN MANAGEMENT

Artificial intelligence is the imitation of human intellect in computers that have been designed to do tasks that would ordinarily require human intelligence. It comprises developing computer systems capable of performing activities such as speech recognition, decision-making, problem-solving, and learning, among others. Machine learning, neural networks, natural language processing, and expert systems are some of the approaches employed by AI technology. Machine learning, a subtype of AI, feeds large amounts of data to algorithms in order for them to learn patterns, make predictions, and improve performance over time. Neural networks, which mimic the structure of the human brain, are used to process and analyze massive volumes of data. AI has found applications in a wide range of industries, including healthcare, banking, transportation, manufacturing, and others [6].

Role of AI in Food Supply Chain Management

Artificial intelligence has the capability of processing and analyzing massive volumes of data, generating correct forecasts, and automating decision-making processes, considerably improving efficiency, quality control, and overall performance in the food supply chain [7]. The AI-based food supply chain tasks shown in Fig. (2) are described below:

Fig. (2))
AI-enabled tasks in the food supply chain.

Demand Forecasting: The power of AI can be leveraged to forecast future demand for food goods. AI analyzes historical sales data, market dynamics, weather patterns, and other relevant parameters to predict the expected demand. This helps in the optimization of production levels, inventory management, and distribution planning in order to satisfy consumer demand while minimizing waste and stock-outs.

Inventory Management: AI algorithms optimize inventory levels by taking into account various factors such as demand projections, lead times, and storage capacity. AI can update reorder points, safety stock levels, and replenishment schedules dynamically to guarantee effective inventory management while lowering carrying costs and decreasing the risk of stock-outs or overstocking.

Quality Control and Inspection: AI-driven computer vision systems that analyze visual data can be utilized to examine food contamination, damage, quality, and safety. AI-assisted quality control and inspection ensure adherence to quality standards by automating the inspection process, enhancing overall product integrity and lowering the probability of providing substandard items.

Supply-Chain Optimization: AI optimizes supply chain operations by analyzing data on routing paths to and from storage facilities, manufacturing schedules, and market demand. Artificial intelligence systems can identify bottlenecks, offer appropriate routes, and dynamically change production and distribution plans to reduce costs as well as improve delivery times.

Shelf Life Prediction: AI algorithms can analyze data such as temperature, humidity, and storage conditions to anticipate the remaining shelf life of perishable food products. This helps to optimize inventory rotation, improve the freshness of food, and reduce wastage.

Supplier and Vendor Management: AI can be incorporated to monitor supplier performance, evaluate vendor dependability, and identify possible risks in the supply chain. AI assists in making informed decisions on supplier selection, contract negotiations, and risk mitigation methods by analyzing data on delivery timeframes and quality issues.

Predictive Analytics for Demand Forecasting

Predictive analytics for demand forecasting is an approach that makes accurate forecasts about future demand for products or services using historical data, statistical algorithms, and machine learning techniques. It entails analyzing patterns, trends, and factors that drive demand in order to provide projections that may guide supply chain decision-making and planning [8]. The method is depicted in Fig. (3).

Data collection is the initial stage in predictive analytics, and it involves gathering relevant historical data on product demand. Sales data, consumer behavior data, market trends, promotional efforts, and external elements such as weather or economic indicators can all be included. The data should be collected over a long enough period to capture seasonal fluctuations, trends, and patterns. Data preprocessing involves cleaning and transformation of the collected data into an appropriate format for analysis. This includes eliminating outliers, dealing with missing numbers, normalizing the data, and aggregating it into relevant time periods (for example, weekly or monthly).

Feature selection is the process of identifying important traits or factors that may influence demand. Historical sales data, price information, marketing efforts, competition data, and other elements that have proven an association with demand patterns might be included.

Fig. (3))
Predictive analytics process for demand forecasting.

Model creation is to develop a demand forecasting model using several statistical and machine learning algorithms on the preprocessed data. Regression analysis, time series analysis and machine learning techniques (e.g., random forest, neural networks) are all popular models. The model is trained on historical data in order to capture trends and correlations between attributes and demand.

Model evaluation and selection involves the assessment of the constructed model by comparing its anticipated outcomes to real demand data from a validation set. To test the model's correctness, performance metrics such as mean absolute error (MAE), root mean square error (RMSE), and mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) are generated. Multiple models can be tried, and the one with the best performance is chosen.

The authors of study [9] suggested a novel demand forecasting model for the hospitality sector that uses Attention-Long Short Term Memory (Attention-LSTM) to anticipate weekly hotel demand four weeks in advance. This approach uses time series demand data in conjunction with additional characteristics derived from K-Means Clustering discoveries, such as the Top 10 Hotel characteristics, and achieves a mean absolute error (MAE) of 4.61 and a mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 23.97.

Two LSTM-based models for demand forecasting in cancer palliative care [10]are the individual-based model and the population-based model. The individual-based model predicts the type and timing of services required for a specific patient based on demographic and health profiles. The population-based model forecasts the overall demand for various services in a care center, helping with resource and operations planning. These models were suggested to estimate demand for a variety of palliative care treatments for patients hospitalized at home with advanced cancer. The individual-based model predicts when a patient will require a service and what sorts of services will be required at that time. The population-based model predicts the number of services that will be sought in the future week for a service center. In forecasting the types of services required by a patient, the individual-based model had an average accuracy of 69.75% and an F1 score of 66.8%. The average MAE of forecasts for institutions No. 1 and No. 2 in the population-based model is 1.07 and 0.6, respectively. Demand forecasts help to optimize inventory management, production scheduling, resource allocation, and supply chain management approaches.

AI-enabled Quality Control and Inspection

The process of AI-driven quality control and inspection in the food supply chain involves utilizing artificial intelligence (AI) technologies, particularly computer vision, to automate and enhance the inspection and assessment of food products for quality, safety, and compliance. A step-by-step explanation of the process is shown in Fig. (4).

Image/Video Capture: The process begins with capturing images or videos of food products using cameras or specialized imaging devices. These images can be taken at various stages of the supply chain, such as during production, processing, packaging, or before shipment.

Preprocessing: The captured images/videos undergo preprocessing to enhance image quality, remove noise, and normalize them for consistent analysis. This step helps to ensure that the images are in a suitable format for AI algorithms to process.

Training Data Collection: A large dataset of labeled images is collected to train the AI model. These labeled images serve as examples for the AI system to learn from and understand different quality attributes, defects, or safety concerns in food products. The dataset is annotated with labels indicating good or acceptable products, as well as specific defects or quality issues.

Fig. (4))
AI-enabled Quality Control and Inspection Process.

Model Development: AI models, particularly deep learning-based neural networks, are trained using the collected dataset. The models learn to identify and classify different quality attributes, defects, or safety concerns in food products based on the patterns and features observed in the training data. Multiple layers within the neural network help in extracting relevant features from the images.

Model Validation and Optimization: The trained model is validated using a separate dataset to assess its performance. Performance metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, and F1 score are calculated to evaluate how well the model can identify and classify different quality issues. The model may undergo optimization and fine-tuning to improve its accuracy and robustness.

Real-Time Inspection: Once the model is validated and optimized, it can be deployed for real-time inspection of food products. New images or videos of food products are inputted into the AI system, which analyzes the visual data using the trained model. The model detects and identifies various quality attributes, defects, or safety concerns present in the products.

Decision-Making and Action: Based on the inspection results, decisions can be made regarding the acceptability of food products. If a defect or quality issue is detected, appropriate actions can be taken, such as segregating the product for further inspection, rejecting it, or triggering a quality control process to rectify the issue. The AI system can provide real-time feedback to operators or trigger alerts for immediate attention.

AI-enabled route optimization and logistics

AI-enabled route optimization and logistics in food supply chain management involves leveraging artificial intelligence (AI) technologies to optimize the movement of goods, streamline transportation routes, and enhance overall logistics operations. By applying AI-enabled route optimization and logistics in food supply chain management, businesses can achieve several benefits, including reduced transportation costs and minimized delays. The steps in AI based route optimization techniques shown in Fig. (5) are described below.

Fig. (5))
Steps in AI-enabled Route Optimization and Logistics.

Data Collection: Relevant data is collected from various sources, including historical shipment data, real-time traffic information, weather conditions, delivery constraints, and customer demands. This data provides insights into transportation patterns, bottlenecks, and other factors that impact logistics operations.

Data Analysis: AI algorithms analyze the collected data to identify patterns, trends, and correlations. This analysis helps uncover hidden relationships between variables, such as optimal routes, delivery times, and factors that affect transportation efficiency. Machine learning techniques can be applied to learn from historical data and make predictions about future logistics operations.

Route Optimization: AI algorithms optimize transportation routes based on multiple factors, including distance, traffic conditions, fuel efficiency, delivery time windows, and vehicle capacities. By considering these variables, the algorithms can generate optimal routes that minimize travel time, reduce fuel consumption, and improve overall efficiency in delivering food products.

Real-time Monitoring: AI systems monitor transportation operations in real time using data from various sources, such as GPS trackers, sensors, and IoT devices. This allows for proactive management of potential disruptions, such as traffic congestion or unexpected delays. Real-time monitoring enables adjustments to routes and schedules, ensuring timely delivery and minimizing delays.

Demand and Supply Matching: AI algorithms can analyze demand patterns, production schedules, and inventory levels to optimize the allocation of resources and match supply with demand. By considering customer demands, delivery constraints, and available inventory, the algorithms can optimize logistics operations to ensure efficient order fulfillment and reduce stockouts or excess inventory.

Load Optimization: AI algorithms optimize the loading of vehicles by considering factors such as product types, weight, volume, and delivery sequence. By intelligently arranging products within the vehicles, the algorithms maximize space utilization, minimize the risk of product damage, and improve delivery efficiency.

Predictive Maintenance: AI can monitor the health and condition of logistics vehicles and equipment using sensor data and historical maintenance records. By analyzing this data, AI algorithms can predict maintenance needs and schedule proactive maintenance activities. Predictive maintenance reduces the risk of breakdowns, improves vehicle reliability, and minimizes disruptions in the supply chain.

LEVERAGING IOT IN FOOD SUPPLY CHAIN MANAGEMENT

Over the years, more attention and activity in supply chain management generally have been noted. Compared to other supply chains, the food supply chain is more complicated and difficult to manage since it has been involved in crucial food conditions like quality of the food, safety issues, and its freshness. The present emerging problem for managing the supply chain is to fulfill the rising customer expectations for food quality as technological advancements alter society, the economy, and consumer behavior. The maintenance of good food quality, which includes other food qualities like safety and freshness, happens to be the most crucial indicator of the effectiveness of the food supply chain. The enhancement of food quality and safety is driven by integrating the food supply chain with quality assurance models and by understanding that food supply chain traceability would ensure food quality, safety, and customer happiness [11].

Introduction to IoT in the Context of Food Supply Chain

One of the most popular innovative technologies now being used in a variety of industrial areas is the Internet of Things (IoT). IoT refers to a collection of physically interconnected digital objects that are used to sense, monitor, and manage supply chain processes. It is observed that over the last ten years, efforts in supply chain research have focused on the Internet of Things. It is realistic to suppose that each food firm will evaluate a variety of variables when deciding whether to implement IoT in its own food supply chain management [12].

IoT-enabled Smart Sensors for Real-time Monitoring

IoT-enabled smart sensors are very much useful for day-to-day applications. These devices are generally termed smart appliances in real time. There are different areas where these smart appliances can be used. This is all because of the smart sensors that have been enabled with IoT. This is what we call the Industrial Revolution 4.0, which includes smart homes, smart cities, smart appliances, smart wearables for health monitoring, real-time transport management, object tracking in space and logistics, smart diagnosis models, etc [13].

In smart cities, the real-time food sector is closely monitored, analyzed, and managed by IoT technology [14]. The implementation of IoT technologies in the food sector enables effective and data-driven operations, enhancing sustainability, productivity, and quality control. The following are some ways that IoT systems assist smart cities in managing and monitoring the food business in real time:

Smart Inventory Management: IoT sensors can be used to continuously track the stock levels of food items. These sensors can monitor variables like temperature, humidity, and expiration dates, enabling better inventory management, waste reduction, and supply chain efficiency [15].

Quality Assurance and Security: IoT devices may continuously monitor and gather information on a variety of variables that affect the nutritional value of food, such as relative humidity, temperature, and storage circumstances. Automated warnings can be set off whenever conditions deviate from ideal ones, enabling prompt remedial action to uphold the quality and safety of food requirements.

Remote Process Monitoring: IoT technology makes it possible to remotely monitor the manufacturing of food. Information about variables like humidity, pressure, temperature, and operational efficiency can be gathered by sensors and connected devices. The proactive maintenance and optimization of production processes are made possible by the real-time analysis of this data to spot any problems or inefficiencies [16].

Optimization of the Supply Chain and Traceability: IoT technology enables seamless traceability in the food supply chain and supply chain optimization. The journey of food products from scratch to the table can be monitored and recorded using sensors that have RFID tags and blockchain-based technologies.

Consumer Engagement and Personalization: By offering individualized recommendations, nutritional data, and real-time information on food availability, IoT devices can improve the consumer experience. For instance, smart refrigerators may keep an eye on the contents and expiry dates of food products and offer recipe or shopping list recommendations depending on the stock of ingredients.

Energy Conservation and Sustainability: IoT systems enable the food business to optimize energy use. Connected devices can track energy consumption and spot places where energy efficiency can be improved, cutting down on waste and reducing emissions. IoT platforms may also analyze data to find trends and patterns that aid in making decisions that will encourage sustainable practices [17].

Tracking and traceability using IoT devices:

IoT devices are essential for improving monitoring and tracing in supply chains because they help companies increase productivity, guarantee the quality and safety of their products, cut waste, and build consumer trust by giving them accurate information about the goods they buy.

Real-time data can be collected and transmitted by IoT devices, allowing for the tracking of product movement and status across the supply chain, as shown in Fig. (6). IoT devices help with tracking and traceability in the following ways:

Fig. (6))
Tracking and traceability using IoT.

Supply Chain Visibility: IoT-enabled tracking solutions give supply chain participants real-time visibility into the whereabouts and status of products. Businesses can track the flow of goods and spot delays or bottlenecks [18, 19].

Monitoring of Temperature and Environmental Conditions: IoT sensors can keep an eye on temperature levels and other outside conditions when transporting and storing perishable items. This information makes sure that products are kept within specific temperature ranges and quality requirements. Automated warnings can be set up if the temperature differs from the appropriate range, enabling prompt steps to stop spoiling or degradation [18, 20].

RFID Tags and Sensors: RFID tags and sensors are frequently employed in tracking systems built on the Internet of Things [21]. These tags and sensors convey information like location, temperature, humidity, and other pertinent data and can be connected to specific products, containers, or pallets. IoT devices continuously record and update this data as goods transit through the supply chain [22].

IoT-based Temperature and Humidity Control

Precision monitoring, self-regulation, remote connectivity, data-driven decision-making, and improved compliance are just a few advantages of IoT-based temperature and humidity management systems. These technologies boost operational effectiveness across a variety of industries, enhance product quality and safety, and consume less energy. Connected equipment and sensors are used by IoT-based humidification and temperature management systems to monitor and manage environmental conditions in a variety of applications, including facilities for cold storage, shipping, and manufacturing processes [23, 24]. IoT offers humidity as well as temperature control in the following ways:

Real-time monitoring: The IoT platform receives the sensor data collection in real time. This makes it possible to continuously monitor the humidity and temperature levels. Users can monitor the data, establish limits, and receive warnings or notifications if conditions fall outside the range that has been established by logging into the application using web interfaces or mobile applications.

Automated Control: IoT devices offer automated control of temperature and humidity conditions. The Internet of Things (IoT) platform can be combined with connected devices like heating and cooling systems, humidification devices, and dehumidifying or HVAC (Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning) systems, as shown in Fig. (7).

Remote Connectivity and Control: Remote accessibility and control is one benefit of IoT-based humidity and temperature control [25]. Utilizing internet-connected devices, users may access the IoT platform from any place to monitor and modify settings.

Analysis of data and insights: Internet of Things (IoT) applications frequently have these features available. To find patterns, trends, and anomalies, information concerning humidity and temperature can be analyzed. These discoveries aid in control strategy optimization, issue detection, and energy efficiency enhancement.

Integration with Other Systems: To increase total efficiency and automation, IoT-based temperature and humidity management platforms can be interconnected with other systems. To ensure optimal storage conditions for perishable commodities, integration with inventory management systems, for instance, can set off alarms or actions based on inventory levels and longevity requirements.

Fig. (7))
Sensors and their Controls.

IoT platforms record previous temperature and humidity data, enabling users to monitor and examine long-term patterns. Additionally, this information may be used to comply with legal obligations, such as those relating to temperature-sensitive goods or showing evidence of conformity when being audited.

BLOCKCHAIN TECHNOLOGY IN FOOD SUPPLY CHAIN MANAGEMENT

Blockchain technology is a distributed ledger system [26, 27] that allows for safe and transparent transaction recording and digital asset management. Blockchain runs through a network of computers called nodes that collaboratively maintain and validate the integrity of the ledger, unlike traditional centralized databases that depend on a single institution for control and verification. The concept of distributed ledger technology lies at the heart of blockchain. It involves a decentralized network where each participant has access to a copy of the ledger, and changes are propagated through consensus mechanisms. As a result of this dispersed structure, no one entity is able to exert total control over the system, which promotes openness and trust among users.

Role of Blockchain Technology in Food Supply Chain Management

When applied to food supply chain management, blockchain technology has several substantial benefits [28]. Blockchain can revolutionize the way we track and control food transportation from farm to fork by exploiting its inherent properties of transparency, traceability, and security. Here are some key benefits:

Transparency: Every transaction and movement of food along the supply chain is recorded on a transparent and immutable blockchain. This transparency enables stakeholders, including consumers, to track the origin, processing, and distribution of food products.

Traceability: Blockchain allows for the seamless tracing of food products from their source to the final destination. Each step of the supply chain, from harvesting or production to packaging, transportation, and retail, can be recorded on the blockchain.

Mitigation of Food Fraud and Counterfeiting: Blockchain technology can help combat food fraud and counterfeiting by establishing an unalterable record of food transactions. By recording every step of the supply chain, including the details of suppliers, distributors, and certifications, blockchain enables easy verification of the authenticity and integrity of food products.

Efficient Supply Chain Management: Blockchain's decentralized and transparent nature enables real-time visibility and information sharing among supply chain participants. This improves coordination, reduces delays, and eliminates paperwork or manual reconciliation processes.

Food Safety and Quality Assurance: Blockchain technology can contribute to ensuring food safety and quality throughout the supply chain. By recording relevant data such as temperature, humidity, and handling procedures at each stage, blockchain helps identify critical control points and potential risks that may impact food safety.

Consumer Trust and Engagement: With access to transparent and reliable information about the food they consume, consumers can make more informed choices. Blockchain empowers consumers to verify claims related to organic certifications, fair trade practices, or sustainable sourcing.

Transparency and Traceability through Blockchain

One of the primary benefits of integrating blockchain technology in food supply chain management is increased transparency and traceability [29]. The decentralized and irreversible ledger of blockchain provides a trustworthy and auditable record of every transaction and movement of food goods, allowing stakeholders to track and trace food from its origin to the consumer [30]. The following characteristics of blockchain ensure transparency and traceability:

Transparent Recordkeeping: Blockchain creates a transparent and shared ledger accessible to all participants involved in the food supply chain, including farmers, manufacturers, distributors, retailers, regulators, and consumers. Every transaction, such as harvesting, processing, packaging, quality testing, and distribution, is recorded on the blockchain in chronological order.

Immutable Transaction History: Once recorded on the blockchain, data cannot be altered or tampered with, ensuring the integrity of the information. This immutability feature prevents fraudulent activities and unauthorized modifications to the records. Stakeholders can rely on the accuracy and authenticity of the information, leading to increased trust among participants and consumers.

End-to-End Traceability: End-to-end traceability of food goods is enabled by blockchain technology, allowing stakeholders to track and verify their origin, quality, and treatment at each level of the supply chain. Every transaction recorded on the blockchain includes relevant information such as product details, batch numbers, certifications, timestamps, and the identities of the parties involved.

Supplier Verification and Compliance: Blockchain facilitates supplier verification and compliance management by securely storing information about suppliers, certifications, and regulatory requirements. With blockchain, stakeholders can easily verify the authenticity and compliance of suppliers, ensuring adherence to ethical sourcing, sustainability, and quality standards.

Consumer Empowerment: Blockchain's transparency and traceability empower consumers to make informed choices about the food they purchase. By scanning QR codes or accessing digital platforms linked to the blockchain, consumers can access detailed information about the product's origin, production methods, ingredients, and safety certifications.

Blockchain-based smart contracts for secure transactions

Blockchain technology, combined with smart contracts [31], offers a powerful solution for secure transactions within the food supply chain. Smart contracts are self-executing agreements with predefined rules encoded on the blockchain. When applied to food supply chain management, blockchain-based smart contracts bring several benefits for secure and efficient transactions.

INTEGRATION OF AI, IOT, AND BLOCKCHAIN IN FOOD SUPPLY CHAIN MANAGEMENT

Integrating AI, IoT, and blockchain technologies can bring numerous benefits and advancements across various industries. When applied to food supply chain management specifically, the integration of these technologies offers several important advantages, such as enhanced transparency, reduced food fraud, real-time monitoring of product conditions, and quick response to supply chain disruptions [32, 33].

There have been several successful pilot projects and initiatives that demonstrate the potential of these technologies. Here are a few notable success stories:

IBM Food Trust: IBM Food Trust is a blockchain-based platform that aims to enhance transparency and traceability in the food supply chain. It enables participants to track food products from farm to table, providing visibility into the origin, processing, and transportation of the products. The platform has been utilized by major companies such as Walmart, Nestlé, and Carrefour to improve food safety, reduce waste, and enhance consumer trust.

Walmart and IBM's Mango Pilot: In collaboration with IBM, Walmart conducted a pilot project that utilized blockchain, IoT, and AI technologies to trace the journey of mangoes from farm to store shelves. IoT sensors were used to monitor temperature and humidity during transportation, while blockchain recorded data at each stage. AI algorithms analyzed the data to identify potential issues and improve the supply chain efficiency.

Alibaba's Blockchain-based Supply Chain Platform: Alibaba, the Chinese e-commerce giant, launched a blockchain-based platform called “Food Trust Framework” to enhance food safety and traceability. The platform integrates IoT devices to collect data on product quality, storage conditions, and logistics. Blockchain technology ensures the immutability and transparency of the recorded data, while AI algorithms analyze the data for quality control and risk assessment.

TE-Food: TE-Food is a blockchain-based food traceability platform that leverages IoT and AI technologies. It tracks the movement of food products from farm to table, collecting data from various IoT devices such as barcodes, RFID tags, and sensors. The data is recorded on the blockchain, ensuring transparency and traceability. AI algorithms analyze the data to provide insights into food safety, quality control, and supply chain optimization. TE-Food has been implemented in several countries, including Vietnam and Hungary, to enhance food traceability and prevent fraud.

LLamasoft: LLamasoft incorporates AI, IoT, and blockchain technologies within its supply chain software solutions to enhance capabilities and provide additional value to its customers. AI is used in LLamasoft's software to leverage advanced analytics and algorithms for various applications. AI enables demand forecasting and planning, optimizing inventory levels, analyzing transportation routes, and identifying patterns or anomalies in supply chain data. By utilizing AI, LLamasoft's software can provide intelligent insights and recommendations to improve decision-making and enhance overall supply chain performance.

CHALLENGES AND RESEARCH DIRECTIONS

The integration of blockchain, IoT, and AI in the food supply chain offers numerous research opportunities. Here are some potential directions for future studies in this field:

Scalability and Performance: Investigating ways to improve the scalability and performance of blockchain networks is a crucial research direction. As the volume of data generated by IoT devices increases, ensuring that blockchain networks can handle large-scale data transactions efficiently is essential.

Privacy and Security: Ensuring the privacy and security of data in the integrated system is a critical concern. Future research can focus on developing robust encryption methods, data anonymization techniques, and secure access control mechanisms. Privacy-preserving AI algorithms that can analyze encrypted data and extract meaningful insights without compromising sensitive information can be explored.

Interoperability and Standards: Investigating interoperability challenges and developing standardized protocols for communication and data exchange between IoT devices, blockchain networks, and AI systems is essential.

Data Fusion and Analytics: Exploring advanced data fusion techniques that integrate data from various IoT devices and other data sources can provide comprehensive insights into the food supply chain. AI algorithms can be further developed to extract actionable information from integrated data sets, enabling real-time decision-making, predictive analytics, and optimization of supply chain operations.

CONCLUSION

In conclusion, the integration of AI, IoT, and blockchain technologies in food supply chain management brings significant benefits and advancements to the industry. By leveraging these technologies, companies can achieve enhanced traceability, improved food safety, increased operational efficiency, and greater transparency throughout the supply chain. AI enables data-driven insights and predictive capabilities, optimizing various aspects of the supply chain, such as demand forecasting, inventory management, and risk identification. IoT devices provide real-time monitoring of critical parameters, ensuring optimal conditions for food products and facilitating end-to-end traceability. Blockchain technology offers a decentralized and tamper-resistant ledger, ensuring transparency, trust, and accountability by recording and verifying each transaction and movement of food products.

Framework Based on IoT, AI, and Blockchain for Smart Access to Government Agricultural Schemes



D. Vinodha1, *, M.J. Buvana2, S. Rajalakshmi2, J. Jenefa1, E.A. Mary Anita1, Maria Lapina3
1 School of Engineering and Technology, CHRIST (Deemed to be University), Kengeri, Bangalore, India
2 Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Sri Venkateswara College of Engineering, Sriperumbudur, India
2 Institute of Digital Development, North-Caucasus Federal University, Stavropol, Russia

Abstract

Agriculture plays an important part in most countries, such as India. A survey says that 54.6% of the total labor force of India is engaged in agriculture and its connected activities. The government is announcing many schemes to facilitate agriculture and support farmers. But most of the farmers are from poor families and are not able to reach the government schemes when they are really in need. Also, it is required to observe and measure the inter and intra-field variability in crops to enjoy the complete benefits of government schemes. This can be done with the advancements in the field of the Internet of Things. Information related to the impact of natural calamities on the agricultural field, malfunctions in the machinery used for cropping, yielding level, and health status of crops can be measured using the technology of IoT (Internet of Things) and analyzed using AI (Artificial Intelligence). Blockchain plays a critical role in replacing traditional means of data storage and exchanging agricultural data with a more trustworthy, immutable, transparent, and decentralized approach. By keeping all the transactions related to government schemes in blockchain, the possible crimes in the form of false data by the intermediate dealers acting between the farmers and the government can be addressed. This, in turn, allows useful government schemes to reach the farmer in time. We propose to develop a theoretical model using IoT, AI, and blockchain, which can assist the farmers in benefitting from the appropriate schemes announced by the government in time and achieving precise agriculture.

Keywords: Artificial intelligence, Blockchain, Drone monitoring system, Internet of Things, Learning model, Multispectral imaging sensors, Precision agriculture, Unmanned aerial vehicles.

* Corresponding author D. Vinodha: School of Engineering and Technology, CHRIST (Deemed to be University), Kengeri, Bangalore, India; E-mail: vinodha.rajkumar@gmail.com

INTRODUCTION

Recently, concerns over food production have increased drastically. It is due to the surge in the world population and the decrease in agricultural resources. Many governments and private organizations are trying to find ways to improve agricultural efficiency. This results in increased research and spending on farming. Nowadays, drones and robots are used for agricultural automation in all stages, from soil preparation to harvesting, to increase productivity and quality. Researchers focus on introducing new approaches to increase yield with fewer agricultural resources. Traditional agriculture uses basic methods of cultivation with primitive knowledge and standard tools. More than half of the world's population uses this method for food production. Even though the cost of production is less in conventional agriculture, it has many cons like low productivity, nutrition depletion, compromise in quality, increase in pollution, etc. Hence, there is a need for some modern techniques in agriculture to improve crop productivity without compromising the quality of the crops.

Precision agriculture uses more accurate ways of cultivation to increase productivity with high quality. It gathers, analyzes, and manages information based on the soil, crops, and other parameters to yield high productivity [1]. It is also known as satellite agriculture. It uses sensors to acquire information about the soil. Real-time images of the crops, farm fields, weather conditions, etc., are acquired using satellites, drones, and robots [2, 3]. Captured images are analyzed further with the sensor data to obtain the necessary information for better cultivation of crops. Agriculture involves many stages, like crop selection, land preparation, seed selection, seed sowing, irrigation, crop growth, fertilizing, and harvesting. Each stage can be automated using drones and robots, which helps in increasing productivity. It also reduces the dependency on weather conditions by creating the best environmental conditions suitable for different crop production. Even though precision farming has many advantages, it is expensive and requires huge capital. It is the main reason that most farmers continue with traditional farming methods. Since over 80% of the farmers are poor, implementing such expensive infrastructure in their small portion of land will be unthinkable for them. In rural areas, there is a difference in the land portions owned by the farmers. Because of the land differences, adapting modern infrastructure is tedious. It also requires prior knowledge to operate the tools, which the farmers must learn. Since they are comfortable with the conventional method, there is a lack of interest in adapting to modern technologies. If they are adopted, understanding and implementing them in the farmlands consume more time and capital. To overcome the drawbacks of precision agriculture mentioned above, the government proposed many schemes to benefit the farmers. To better understand the proposed government schemes, a friendly handbook explaining them and their benefits has also been issued to the farmers by the Department of Agriculture, Cooperation and Farmers Welfare (DAC&FW) and the Ministry of Agriculture and Farmers Welfare. It includes the details of the assistance provided to the farmers to increase productivity. A few details are listed below.

• Assistance to improve soil conditions by distributing micronutrients and soil ameliorants and supplying gypsum/pyrite/lime/dolomite, lime/liming materials, and bio-fertilizers, which are proposed with affordable prices for the benefit of the farmers.

• Water management methods under the Mission for Sustainable Agriculture (MSA) include drip irrigation, sprinkler irrigation, water harvesting systems for individuals, construction of tube wells/bore wells (shallow/medium), etc.

• E-marketing of agriculture commodities.

• Assistance with organic farming with bio-chemicals, bio-pesticides, and bio-fertilizers.

• Assistance in the cultivation of horticulture crops.

Though the government has proposed many schemes to help the farmers achieve high productivity, they have not been well received by the farmers. On the other hand, because of the intermediate dealers, the farmers are not getting their actual share for their contribution, which in turn reduces the farmers' trust in government schemes, which may also lead to conflicts, protests, and other severe consequences. Thus, a secure system is needed to exchange the information directly with the farmers rather than through the intermediate dealer. With the system, the farmers can acquire benefits and productivity can be increased. Hence, this paper proposes a model with recent technologies to assist the farmers in getting benefits from the schemes. The paper is organized as follows: Section 2 explicates the existing precision agriculture methods with their merits and demerits. Section 3 describes the technologies used in various stages of farming to increase productivity. A framework of smart access model for accessing government schemes is proposed in Section 4, and Section 5 concludes this paper.

EXISTING SYSTEMS

With rising population, the problem pertaining to food production has attracted focus. Population with diverse needs is expected to increase to between 9.4 and 10.1 billion by 2050, increasing the need for designated areas for food production [4]. Human-induced environmental changes may result in circumstances where the growth of new crops is impossible. In the same way, increasing urbanization raises prices, decreases productivity, and reduces labor in regions that are normally involved in food production [5]. Smart agriculture is a novel concept of agriculture management that applies technical mechanisms at several stages of farming production to address the difficulties associated with the demands for food production and the decline in the workforce [6, 7]. It makes use of sensing nodes, communication networks, management information systems, and data analysis tools [8].

The term “Internet of Things” (IoT) is frequently used to define this network of connected gadgets [9]. By enabling the relevant activities to be taken at the proper time, quantity, and location, the data generated by smart farming aids in increasing output and reducing waste [10]. Additionally, recent technology advancements in IoT-related fields make it simpler to implement and use smart agriculture with IoT [11]. These technical advancements comprise network connectivity, hardware size reduction, power usage optimization, and device cost reduction. Additionally, through developing encouragement programs and strategies to support research and training financially, the largest agricultural producers in the world are pushing the use of IoT in smart farming [12].

IoT solutions for smart agriculture are continually gaining new contributions and improving and are extensively reviewed in several studies [13, 14]. The authors provide a number of use cases for the cited IoT applications for smart farming. The authors of a study [15] recognized data visualization techniques and edge computing and communication technologies within these fields. The IoT solutions for smart farming and smart agriculture and the role of sensing devices in the various IOT-based applications with three-layer architecture are analyzed in research studies [16, 17].

IoT-based real-time agriculture monitoring systems for upgrading irrigation systems and measuring soil moisture in a field have been suggested in several research works [18, 19]. To enable farmers to choose wisely based on a prepared decision table, they separated the supervised field into tiny sectors and examined the information gathered by the sensing nodes sector-wise. These investigations demonstrated effectiveness in terms of data compression, energy preservation, and precise judgment. Agriculture is important for long-term financial growth and organizational change in terms of the preparation, creation, and transportation of domesticated animals and crops. At the moment, cutting-edge IoT, WiFi, machine learning, and AI technologies [20] are having an impact on the agriculture sector. With the aid of these technologies, data collection and analysis on factors like temperature, weather, soil characteristics, and previous crop performance can now be used to predict future crop performance and address issues with weed management, crop diseases, pesticide control, and water management [21, 22]. An artificial neural network-based (ANN) model is proposed in a study [23] to predict the formation of frost using parallel reasoning. Another model [24] distinguishes cotton weeds from cotton plants and soil with an accuracy of 92%. In another study [25], the ANN model for predicting water resource variables and the issues to be considered while developing the learning models are presented. In a research work [26], a model based on ANN and an expert system to identify crop nutritional disorders is proposed. A model to monitor soil moisture using a transceiver equipped with UAV is proposed in a study [27]

An expert system using fuzzy logic for the precision agriculture of soya beans [28] enables farmers to get the correct assistance from an agricultural expert in time. Another fuzzy-based model [29] is implemented to grade the leaf disease automatically by segmenting the color image and computing the total leaf area and illness area. An inference system using fuzzy logic is created [30] to predict plant diseases based on weather data. The “IF, THEN” condition is used to signal diseases only when the measured temperature and humidity value falls within a specific range. An IOT-based expert system helps to increase productivity [31] and reduce the loss due to disease by making informed decisions.

Due to the lack of readily accessible trustworthy information on the origins of food goods, the current systems also lack customer trust [32]. Consequently, blockchain technology is a helpful tool for offering a practical fix to the aforementioned problems. Since every node (user) linked to the blockchain has a replica of every transaction, these details kept on a blockchain cannot be changed or deleted. As a result, both the transaction and validity may be independently checked. Numerous existing technologies for data collection, storage, and distribution are integrated with blockchain technology [33]. The autonomous nature allows the nodes in the network to be homologous and not bound to the network [34]. Blockchain enables the reading, validating, and storing of a shared ledger of transactions in a chain of blocks [35]. Smart agriculture systems, which are more efficient than e-agriculture systems, include sensor technology like GPS and IoT, cloud computing, and mobile technologies [36]. Additionally, IoT-enabled farm management systems are easily intercepted by hackers, who can then alter the data. The key characteristics of blockchain technology can address e-agricultural systems' operational issues for smart agriculture [37]. The food supply networks and agricultural productivity have significantly improved thanks to the agricultural systems. Many scholars and organizations are considering agriculture 4.0 [38-40], which incorporates big data, blockchain, IoT, and precision agriculture for all facets of sustainable agriculture. In order to preserve sustainable agricultural practices and promote healthy crop growth, farmers can use these technologies to observe and analyze the data.

According to Zhang [33], blockchain technology in agriculture could streamline the credit-checking and financing process. To realize its full potential, blockchain technology must be incorporated into the already-existing legacy systems and IoT-based systems. Its adoption is constrained by technological obstacles such as infrastructure management, scalability, interoperability, and excessive power consumption. However, blockchain has the potential to be utilized for financial transactions, government-to-business and business-to-business transactions, as well as secure machine-to-machine communication between IoT devices, enabling smart and sustainable agriculture even on a big scale.

TECHNOLOGIES USED

Emerging technologies like Artificial Intelligence (AI), blockchain, drones, the Internet of Things (IoT), and big data analytics can raise productivity and effectiveness at all points in the agricultural value chain [41-46]. This can increase farmers' incomes, increase farm productivity while decreasing waste, and improve supply-chain efficiency, transparency, and sustainable resource use.

IoT in Precision Agriculture

IoT devices and sensors play a significant role in precision agriculture by gathering data in real time on numerous aspects of crop development and environmental conditions [47, 48]. To make educated judgments about irrigation, fertilization, pest management, and other agricultural practices, the data gathered by these devices and sensors is analyzed. Sample examples of the various IoT gadgets and sensors employed in precision agriculture are discussed below.

Detectors of Soil Moisture

By measuring soil moisture levels, these sensors help farmers optimize irrigation and avoid overwatering [49, 50]. To establish the most effective irrigation plan, they can either be embedded directly in the soil or used in conjunction with weather sensors [51]. Fig. (1) shows the soil moisture sensor.

Weather Monitoring Sensor

Dynamic variation of environmental conditions is a major factor influencing the accuracy of sensor data [53]. Sensor nodes deployed in the crop field are vulnerable to dynamic environments, like rainfall, temperature instabilities, variations in wind speed, daylight, and other aspects. A major open challenge that calls for the research community to put in significant effort is the advance of new approaches for atmospheric modification, cloud discovery, and sound interpolation. It can gauge temperature, humidity, wind speed, and other elements of the environment that may have an impact on crop growth. Farmers can modify their farming practices by gathering information on these issues.

Fig. (1))
Soil Moisture Sensor [52].

Sensors for Crop Health

DHT11 and LDR, along with camera and soil moisture sensors [54], can measure a plant's health by looking for indications of disease, pest, and nutritional shortage. This enables farmers to identify difficulties early and take appropriate action before they worsen.

GPS Trackers

Precision planting is one of the main advantages of GPS tracking in agriculture [55]. Fig. (2) shows the GPS tracker. Fields can be mapped out using GPS monitoring technology, enabling farmers to sow crops precisely and effectively. Crop yields may increase as a result of more effective use of resources like water and fertilizer. It enables farmers to optimize their processes and increase efficiency by tracking the movement of livestock and farm equipment.

Drone

Small, autonomous aircraft, known as drones, are employed in agriculture to support field management, precision farming, and crop monitoring [56]. On crop health, soil quality, irrigation, fertilizer, and pest management, they can offer real-time data [57-59]. They can also cut down on operating expenses, pollution, and labor costs. Farmers are using drones more frequently as they provide a more effective and economical means of managing crops. Fig. (3) illustrates the usage of drones in field monitoring.

Fig. (2))
GPS Trackers [60].
Fig. (3))
Drone [61].

Role of AI in Precision Agriculture

Precision agriculture benefits greatly from artificial intelligence (AI), which gives farmers accurate, up-to-date information on their crops and soil conditions. To offer information on crop growth, nutritional levels, and insect infestations, AI-powered technologies may analyze data from sensors, drones, and satellites. This knowledge aids farmers in making data-driven decisions about insect control, fertilization, and irrigation that increase yields and decrease waste. Additionally, AI can help predict weather patterns, which enables farmers to modify their planting and harvesting schedules as necessary. Overall, AI in precision agriculture can assist farmers in streamlining their processes, minimizing their negative effects on the environment, and boosting profitability [62-64]. India must confront risks like supply chain disruptions, pandemics, and climate change in addition to the issue of feeding a growing population. By encouraging the application of artificial intelligence and other technologies, the Indian agricultural industry is revolutionized. The technology is being used by almost 7,000 farmers to examine the soil and monitor crop quality and health.

Role of Blockchain in Precision Agriculture

Blockchain technology can be utilized to gather data about the quality of seed, supervise crop growth, and trace a crop's path after it leaves the farm. If blockchains are properly validated and put into use, they can stop unethical and unlawful production and distribution that threatens sustainability and local food security [65-67]. In order to ensure user trust, traditional blockchains are decentralized and democratized. Small-scale farmers who lack the necessary size, scale, and technological know-how to take part may also be excluded if corporations control the knowledge about the supply chain. Many academics contend that both large and small farms are necessary to sustain the world's expanding population and that the gap between large and small-scale food manufacturers can contribute to global food uncertainty. A number of issues need to be addressed before blockchain and other information technologies can help with food security. Blockchain adoption has to be distributed to include small farmers and rural residents [68]. As a result, consumers will be able to make educated decisions, and sustainable and equitable food systems will be enabled. However, given the added responsibility that blockchains place on the end user, issues like low digital literacy among the world's poor and infrastructure limitations can break true decentralization. In order to make them sensitive to social and environmental values necessary to address food insecurity among various groups, they must also be incorporated into larger food security promotion efforts. By combining the Internet of Things and blockchain technology, smart agriculture can take the form of a network of smart farms with increasing influence over supply chains [69]. This combination will produce a greater degree of sovereignty and cleverness in the management of precision agriculture in ways that are more effective and optimized.

PROPOSED MODEL

In this chapter, we propose a model based on IoT, AI, and blockchain for smart access to government schemes by farmers. The various stages in the construction of the proposed model are discussed in this section.

Drone Monitoring System (DMS)

Drones are considered the most promising technology that has revolutionized the various operations involved in agriculture [70]. Drones are otherwise known as Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs). These Smaller UAVs with greater cargo capacity are now conceivable because of advancements in embedded systems and motor technology. Drones may be launched almost instantaneously and can help with remote coverage of the affected areas without the need for operations or safety planning. The aerial view of the crops affected by natural calamities like floods, storms, and bad weather can be observed with the help of drone monitoring systems. Using drones, images of the affected crops are collected, providing spectral information in the infrared and visual ranges. The affected fields can be efficiently explored and the environmental impact can be assessed using DMS. Because of the drone, the images of the affected field can be collected from the desired location with the desired height at the desired frequency. This helps in making the assessment more precise. Fig. (4) shows the components of the drone monitoring system. The DMS is empowered by incorporating the power of an artificial intelligence-based machine learning model. The spatial variability in the field due to calamities can be assessed with the help of a trained learning model using the images captured by drones.

Fig. (4))
Drone monitoring system.

Soil Health Monitoring System

Soil health is monitored remotely using sophisticated image processing technology along with the UAV loaded with remote sensing cameras. The information required for assessing the health of the soil is collected by measuring the electromagnetic spectrum of light that is reflected from the surface soil. Scaled maps of the conditions across wide expanses of farmland are produced using multispectral photography. The elements present in the soil are different from each other in how they reflect wavelengths of light. Multispectral imaging sensors are used for collecting piles of data about these wavelengths. The gathered information is fed into a machine-learning model for analysis. Using the digital mapping of soil classification, the physical and chemical properties of soil can be assessed efficiently. These findings are recorded as a chain of blocks in encrypted form along with time spatial details. Fig. (5) illustrates the process of creating blocks of information required for analyzing the soil health. The DMS takes the spatiotemporal data from a chain of blocks and compares the impact of calamities on the health of the soil. If the impact is above the threshold level that can be fixed by a human expert from the authorized agency, then the farmer of the corresponding field is considered to be eligible for the scheme. A new block with this identified scheme details, along with the farmer ID, is created and added to the blockchain of farmer details.

Fig. (5))
Creation of Block – Soil Health.

Crop Health Monitoring System

We propose to measure the impact of natural calamities on the health of crops using the stream of images taken from drones. The first vital factor to be assessed is a rise in the water level due to rain and flood. Next, the impact of calamities on the health of crops is analyzed using the same multispectral image data collected from the drone and AI-based classification models.

To measure the depth of water in the field due to flood, a drone mounted with ultrasonic sensors is flown over the field to be monitored. The distance between the sensor and the field surface is measured using the following equation.

(1)

Where ‘d’ is the distance of the object, ‘t’ is the time taken for the sound wave to reach the receiver, and ‘s’ is the speed of the sound, which is 343 meters per second. This stream of data (di, li) coming out of the sensor along with spatial information (li) is fed into the trained model. The training model extracts information related to the depth of water level and crop health using the data received from the sensors. This is illustrated in Fig. (6).

Fig. (6))
Creation of block - crop health.

Health Assessing Model

The information used for analyzing the health of soil and crops is available as a sequence of blocks in the blockchain BC_DMS(Blockchain for DMS). The health assessing model is fed with information about the soil and crop along with the details of calamities. The impact on the crop’s health due to flood is assessed based on the level of water information and duration of water logging and calamities detail. The average height of the crop is measured by comparing the distance between the crop and the water, which is sensed by the ultrasonic sensor. If the distance is less than the threshold value, the impact level is classified based on the water level. The multispectral image is analyzed by the training model, and the health status of the crop is classified. A convolutional neural network-based learning model with two hidden layers is considered to be the best model for the proposed scheme.

Blockchains for Smart Access to Government Schemes

To enable farmers to access the eligible schemes in time with no delay, the information associated with the health status of soil and crops is maintained in a blockchain called BC_DMS. To track the status of scheme utilization by eligible farmers, a separate blockchain called BC_FSS (BC for Field-wise Sanctioned schemes) is maintained.

Blockchain for DMS (BC_DMS)

The details related to the health status of soil and crops are precisely identified using the machine learning model from the images gathered by the drones at the desired height at desired locations. The identified features are grouped in blocks along with time, location, and FID (Field ID). This block is added to the chain of BC_DMS.

BC for Field-wise Sanctioned Schemes (BC_FSS)

Based on the details available in the BC_DMS, the health status of soil and crop is assessed using AI-based trained machine model. The models identify the suitable schemes for which the farmer is an eligible beneficiary. A block that carries the information related to the eligible schemes, scheme details, and the corresponding FID is created. This block is chained with the BC_FSS blockchain. The construction process of the BC_FSS blockchain is illustrated in Fig. (7).

Fig. (7))
Construction of BC_FSS blockchain

Smart Contracts

The main objective of the proposed framework is to show the usage of blockchain in tracing the fund allocation to eligible farmers based on the schemes identified and stored in the BC_FSS. It also aims to monitor the farmer's utilization of the allocated funds. This is implemented using the smart contract written in the programming language called Solidity and the work is based on using Ethereum-based blockchain technology. The proposed framework can be implemented using the Remix development environment. For tracing the fund allocation and utilization, the contract makes use of attributes, operational functions, and events. The contract, named “sanction amount verification”, is proposed with the required attributes, functions, and events, as shown in Fig. (8). It verifies whether the amount transferred to the farmer’s account is the same as the amount sanctioned using the function Transaction_checking (). The function uses the details of sanctioned schemes like the amount, farmers’ account number, scheme, etc., which are available in the BC_FSS. The authenticity of the transaction is verified using the contract function called InitateTrancation().I It ensures whether the amount is transferred from the authorized office and received by the farmer to whom the amount is sanctioned. Another contract called utilization checker is used for verifying and tracing whether the sanctioned amount is utilized properly by the farmer for the development of the crop field. The required attributes, functions, and events are given in Fig. (9). Once utilization details are uploaded by the farmer, the verification () function checks the authenticity and transfers this to the approval process. The approval function validates the details furnished by the farmer by analyzing the health status of the field received from the field sensor. The overall architecture of the proposed model is shown in Fig. (10). Once the schemes are identified based on the level of impact on the crops and soil due to calamities, the farmers and agents can access the details from the blockchain. The enciphered nature of blockchain prevents the unauthorized modification of details related to the health status of crops and approved schemes. Also, it ensures transparency in the sanctioning of the government schemes and allows the farmers to taste the benefits of appropriate schemes in time.

Fig. (8))
Amount sanction verification contract.
Fig. (9))
Amount sanction verification contract.
Fig. (10))
Architecture of the Smart Access Model for Government Schemes.

CONCLUSION

The government has taken a lot of initiatives and announced various schemes for the welfare of farmers who are the backbone of most developing countries. Since most of the farmers are living in remote rural areas, the schemes announced by the government, especially during natural calamities like drought, flood, and cyclones, do not reach the farmers in time. Our proposed model automatically and precisely measures the level of impact on crops and soil due to natural calamities with the help of IoT-enabled machine-learning models. It identifies the schemes based on the level of impact and sanctions of the scheme. This enables the farmers to enjoy the benefits of government schemes without delay. In addition, since the level of impact is analyzed by the machines with less intervention of humans, human-made mistakes and corruption are minimized. Also, all necessary transactions related to the schemes are automatized and maintained as a block in a blockchain without the intervention dealers. This minimizes the possible crimes related to the involvement of intermediated dealers between the government and farmers.
