Transforming Agriculture with IoT for Precision Agriculture and Sustainable Crop Management



Suyash Bhardwaj1, *, Sasirekha Venkatesan2, Swati Rawat3, Pashupati Nath4
1 Department of Computer Science Engineering, Gurukula Kangri Deemed to be University, Uttarakhand, India
2 Department of Management Studies, Sri Sairam Engineering College, Chennai, India
3 MMICT&BM, Maharishi Markandeshwar Deemed to be University, Haryana, India
4 College of Smart Agriculture, COER University, Uttarakhand, India

Abstract

The Internet of Things (IoT) technology is making a radical transition in the agricultural business, resulting in the creation of precision agriculture and sustainable crop management practices. This study inspects how Internet of Things (IoT) technology is revolutionizing agriculture, with a particular emphasis on sustainable crop management techniques and precision agriculture. The study explores the extent and significance of using sensors, IoT devices, and data analytics for improved crop monitoring and management, empowering farmers to make data-driven choices. Farmers are able to allocate resources more efficiently and produce less waste due to the real-time data collecting on soil moisture, temperature, humidity, and crop health. We go into great detail on the essential elements of IoT-based precision agriculture, such as decision support systems, data collecting, analytics, and sensor technology. The study also looks at the benefits of using IoT in agriculture, highlighting how technology might completely transform farming methods for more sustainability and efficiency. A thorough literature study adds to our understanding of the status of research in Internet of Things applications for sustainable crop management and precision agriculture.

Keywords: Artificial intelligence (AI), Internet of things (IoT), IoT in agriculture, Precision agriculture, Sustainable crop management.

* Corresponding author Suyash Bhardwaj: Department of Computer Science Engineering, Gurukula Kangri Deemed to be University, Uttarakhand, India; Tel: +91-9719580167; E-mail: Suyash.bhardwaj@gmail.com

INTRODUCTION TO IOT IN AGRICULTURE

The interconnection of physical things, automobiles, structures, and other items that are merged with software, sensors, electronics, and connections to networks, allowing them to collect and transmit data, is referred to as the Internet of Things

(IoT). The purpose of IoT is to build a seamless communication network between items and systems, allowing the development of smart arrangements capable of collecting, treating, and analyzing data in order to make choices and conduct actions [1, 2].

The Internet of Things (IoT) idea goes back to the late 1980s when academics first began investigating the possibility of linking common things to the internet. However, the technology and infrastructure required to execute IoT did not become accessible until the 2000s. IoT has become a fast-developing industry, with applications in a variety of businesses, such as agriculture, as a result of broad internet usage and the growth of low-cost devices and wireless communication technologies [3].

The usage of IoT in agriculture has developed quickly in the past few years as farmers seek ways to enhance efficiency, lower costs, and raise yields. IoT-based agriculture systems have been utilized to automate operations such as crop and soil health monitoring, irrigation system control, and animal tracking. IoT in agriculture has also enabled the assembly and examination of vast sizes of data, giving farmers useful insights into their operations [4]. The application of IoT technology and systems to improve agricultural practices and boost efficiency is referred to as IoT in agriculture. This may be accomplished by collecting data on soil health, crop growth, weather patterns, and other elements that affect agricultural operations using sensors, cloud computing, and other IoT-based skills. This data may then be evaluated to help farmers make better decisions and improve agricultural practices, resulting in higher yields, lower costs, and a more sustainable agriculture business [5, 6].

This research attempts to highlight the most recent trends and scientific findings in the fields of IoT and agriculture. According to Google Trends, there has been a significant increase in searches for the terms precision agriculture and sustainable crop management. Precision agriculture and sustainable crop management have had varying degrees of popularity. The chart below depicts the web search prevalence for the aforementioned terms as determined by Google search trends since 2024.

This chapter provides a widespread outline of the Internet of Things (IoT) in agriculture. The chapter begins with an introduction to IoT, outlining the concept and underlining its many agricultural applications. The chapter goes on to discuss the advantages of installing IoT-based agricultural systems. It then looks into the many types of IoT sensors used in agricultural, including soil moisture sensors, crop sensors, weather sensors, animal sensors, and irrigation sensors. The sections that follow go into further depth on IoT-based data collection and management systems, covering both centralized and decentralized approaches. It talks about cloud-based data management systems as well as the emerging trend of edge computing in IoT-based agriculture, underlining the need of real-time data processing and localized decision making. The chapter focuses on IoT-based precision agriculture, providing both an explanation and a summary of the advantages of employing precision agricultural practices. The chapter then goes into IoT-based sustainable agriculture management, such as crop monitoring, automated irrigation systems, and pest and disease management using IoT technologies. Finally, the chapter reviews the key findings and examines the importance of IoT in transforming agriculture into more efficient, sustainable, and technologically driven agricultural practices. The references section includes a comprehensive list of all sources and publications cited throughout the chapter, making it an invaluable resource for agricultural researchers, practitioners, and policymakers interested in the possibilities of IoT.

Definition of IoT

The Internet of Things (IoT) is a collection of physical objects, including automobiles, buildings, and other items, that are connected to the internet and equipped with software, hardware, sensors, and network connections. The purpose of IoT is to build a seamless communication network between items and systems, allowing the development of smart organizations capable of collecting, processing, and analyzing data in order to make choices and conduct actions [7, 8]. The Internet of Things (IoT) is the convergence of a number of technologies, including artificial intelligence (AI), ubiquitous computing, inexpensive sensors, and increasingly potent embedded systems [9]. Several instances include healthcare delivery systems, cameras, fixtures for lighting, heating and cooling systems, security systems for homes, and other products [10].

Applications of IoT in Agriculture

According to current estimates, 9.6 billion people will populate the world by 2050. In order to feed this massive population, agriculture is being compelled to use the Internet of Things. IoT is eradicating concerns such as harsh weather, climatic shifts, and environmental degradation while also supporting us in addressing the growing need for food [11]. Previously, mechanical innovations such as tractors were introduced for managing the ever-growing food demand. Many more sophisticated sensors are being used in agriculture as a result of the development of industrial IoT. A mobile or satellite communication network currently connects the sensors to the cloud. It enables us to get sensor data instantly and make defensible decisions.

IoT tenders in the agriculture industry have significantly improved irrigation systems by enabling real-time monitoring of water tank echelons. The widespread use of sensors throughout the agricultural process has allowed for precise tracking of crop development and resource utilization [12, 13]. The benefits of IoT in agriculture are evident, as it has facilitated effective water utilization, optimized inputs, and revolutionized farming practices. IoT-based smart farming [14] continually monitors the land, reducing excessive resource usage and saving farmers time through sensor-based adjustments in humidity, temperature, and soil conditions. Data collection on rainfall, humidity, temperature, infestations of insects, and composition of the soil are just a few of the many IoT uses in agriculture. This data enables automation, informed decision-making, waste reduction, risk management, and efficient crop management [15, 16]. Farmers can remotely monitor soil conditions and utilize IoT data to create precise fertilization plans, enhancing the overall agricultural system.

The ultimate objective is to integrate information from sensors with the farmers' understanding and instinct about his or her private property in order to boost agricultural output while lowering expenses. There are several IoT applications in agriculture, but just a handful are shown in Fig. (1), with a brief summary of each application.

Fig. (1))
Google Trends reaction for the terms “Precision Agriculture” and “Sustainable Crop Management” since 2004.
Fig. (2))
Application of IoT in agriculture.

Some of the applications of IoT in agriculture are as follows:

Precision agriculture

Utilizing innovations like the Internet of Things (IoT)-based sensors for precise monitoring and control, precision agriculture applies science and technology to manage spatial and temporal variability in agricultural output. This allows farmers to increase yields and make well-informed decisions while maintaining environmental sustainability [17].

Agricultural Drones

In comparison to traditional land vehicles, drones cover areas that are ten to fifteen times larger and come with computer-controlled automation for tasks like weather investigation, crop disease monitoring, and soil quality assessment. These drones are revolutionizing industries like agriculture and improving crop management [18, 19].

Automated irrigation

Precision agriculture reduces water waste and increases crop yields using automated irrigation systems that use Internet of Things (IoT) sensors to monitor soil humidity, weather, and environmental variables. These systems optimize water distribution to crops based on real-time data. Numerous varieties, including pneumatic, portable timer, hybrid timer/sensor, SCADA, and paddock control modules, provide a range of methods for effectively managing irrigation, remotely modifying structures, and even regulating pumps and motors to enhance water resource conservation [20, 21].

Livestock Monitoring

Using temperature, weight, and activity sensors, an Internet of Things-enabled livestock monitoring system tracks and manages sheep, pigs, chickens, and cattle. It analyzes data to optimize care, keep an eye on health and behavior, and automate feeding and watering schedules in an effort to improve animal productivity, efficiency, and well-being in general [22].

Crop Monitoring System

The IoT-based crop monitoring system offers crucial insights into crop growth and enables scientific advice and preventative steps in the face of fluctuating global weather conditions [23, 24]. Through an internet-connected sensor network, an agricultural monitoring system gathers and analyses data to optimize practices like irrigation, fertilization, and pest control, with the aim of improving crop yield, efficiency, and sustainability. It does this by using IoT sensors for temperature, moisture, and light [25, 26].

Smart Greenhouses

Smart greenhouses, powered by IoT, automate and optimize environmental factors for precise crop management, reducing water use by up to 70%. Remote monitoring enhances efficiency, preventing diseases, and minimizing energy consumption [27, 28]. Proper greenhouse design and orientation, emphasizing energy efficiency, contribute to uniform crop quality and increased nutritional value [29, 30].

Environmental Monitoring

IoT-based real-time environment tracking uses sensors for humidity, temperature, light, and soil moisture, enabling efficient data collection and remote monitoring. This technology benefits agriculture and other industries, optimizing output, conserving resources, and improving efficiency and profitability [31].

Remote Sensing

IoT-based remote sensing with strategically placed devices monitors crops in real time, aiding farmers in taking prompt action to prevent diseases, track crop development, and make informed decisions on weather patterns and soil quality for precise irrigation and cultivation [32, 33].

Supply Chain Management

IoT in agriculture utilizes monitoring devices for an efficient supply chain, reducing waste. Farmers benefit from valuable data, enabling informed decisions, automation, and increased productivity, fostering sustainability [34].

Advantages of IoT-based agriculture systems

IoT-based agriculture systems provide a number of benefits that can increase agricultural operations' efficiency, profitability, and sustainability [35]. Among the primary advantages are:

Improved Efficiency: IoT-based solutions automate processes like crop and soil health monitoring, irrigation system control, and animal tracking, decreasing the need for manual labor and enhancing efficiency.

Enhanced Yields: IoT-based agriculture systems give farmers useful insights into their operations by collecting and analyzing data on soil health, crop development, and other aspects, allowing them to make educated decisions and enhance yields.

Cost Savings: IoT-based technologies automate operations, eliminating the need for manual labor and boosting efficiency, resulting in cost savings for farmers.

Increased Profitability: IoT-based agriculture solutions can boost farmers' profits by increasing yields and lowering expenses.

Improved Sustainability: IoT-based solutions may be utilized to decrease waste, preserve resources, and improve agricultural enterprises' sustainability.

Better Decision-Making: IoT-based systems gather and analyze vast volumes of data, giving farmers important insights into their operations and helping them to make informed decisions and enhance efficiency.

IoT-based solutions enable real-time tracking and monitoring of crops, soil health, animals, and other aspects, allowing farmers to respond rapidly to any changes or difficulties.

Fig. (3))
Advantages of IoT-based agriculture systems.

IoT-based agriculture systems provide a number of benefits that can increase agricultural operations' efficiency, profitability, and sustainability [12, 35]. IoT-based technologies are revolutionizing the agriculture business by automating jobs, gathering and analyzing data, and delivering important insights into agricultural operations.

IOT SENSORS IN AGRICULTURE

IoT sensors are devices that collect data on a variety of agricultural activities, including soil health, crop growth, weather patterns, and livestock health [36]. The goal of IoT sensors in agriculture is to provide farmers with meaningful data and insights into their processes, enabling them to make informed decisions and improve the productivity, sustainability, and efficiency of their operations.

Types of IoT sensors used in agriculture

Many IoT sensors are utilized in agriculture to gather information on a variety of agricultural processes, including soil quality, crop development, weather patterns, and livestock health. Here are some examples of common Internet of Things (IoT) sensors used in agriculture, along with an approximation of their costs, features, and applications.:

Soil Moisture Sensors

Soil moisture sensors detect soil moisture and temperature, supporting irrigation management and crop monitoring to prevent over- and under-watering. These electronic tools are essential for farmers and gardeners, ensuring the right water supply for crops and preventing crop failure when integrated with irrigation systems. Common types include capacitance, tensiometer, and temporal domain reflectometry (TDR) sensors [37]. Capacitance sensors detect soil moisture by gauging changes in dielectric constant, tensiometers measure soil humidity tension, and TDR sensors assess moisture through electrical pulse travel time [37, 38]. Used in horticulture, agriculture, landscaping, and environmental monitoring, these sensors enhance productivity, prevent overwatering, reduce water waste, and improve irrigation efficiency, also measuring soil temperature, salinity, and nutrient content [38, 39].

Fig. (4))
Soil Moisture Sensor.

Crop Sensors

Crop sensors capture data on crop health, growth, and metrics like nitrogen content, aiding informed agricultural decision-making and enabling timely action for stress or disease prevention [40, 41]. Active and passive models emit or monitor radiation, generating maps for precise input applications in agriculture, reducing costs, increasing yields, and minimizing environmental impact [41].

Fig. (5))
Crop sensor.

Weather Sensors

Weather sensors monitor temperature, humidity, and wind speed, providing essential data for precision agriculture [42, 43]. Installed on farms, these devices optimize irrigation, predict weather patterns, and conserve resources. Integrated into central networks, they offer real-time data access and remote monitoring, that is especially beneficial for farmers with remote locations or multiple farms [43, 44]. Various sensor types, including thermometers, barometers, anemometers, hygrometers, and rain gauges, can be used individually or combined for comprehensive environmental data, often integrated with GPS for more precise information [44].

Livestock Sensors

Livestock sensors optimize cattle welfare by gathering data on health, behavior, and productivity, reducing disease risks [45, 46]. Continuously monitoring aspects such as body temperature, activity levels, feeding patterns, and reproduction rate, these devices integrated into central networks enable remote monitoring, improving productivity and animal welfare while reducing medication usage.

Fig. (6))
Weather sensors.
Fig. (6))
Weather sensors.
Fig. (7))
Livestock sensor.

Irrigation Sensors

Irrigation sensors optimize water usage and enhance agricultural output by measuring soil moisture levels and connecting to irrigation systems for remote control and water conservation [47]. As crucial components of IoT-based farming systems, these devices provide essential data and insights, enabling farmers to improve efficiency, profitability, and sustainability while conserving resources [48]. The market provides a number of irrigation sensor types, each of which is designed to evaluate a certain environmental parameter. The following are the most common types of irrigation sensors:

Soil moisture sensors:These sensors allow farmers to change irrigation schedules by determining how much water is available in the soil. They can be positioned at different soil depths to provide more exact information on the soil's moisture levels at different depths.

Weather sensors:They are utilized to measure environmental variables, including hotness and moistness, wind speed, and solar radiation. Farmers can use them to anticipate weather trends and make adjustments to irrigation plans as needed [49].

Flow sensors: These devices monitor the flow of water via an irrigation system, allowing farmers to detect leaks and ensure that the water is distributed consistently over the field. By measuring the volume of water lost by evaporation and transpiration from the soil and plants, evapotranspiration sensors let farmers adjust irrigation schedules to fit the water demands of their crops.

Irrigation sensors can be linked to a central network that provides real-time data and allows farmers to remotely monitor their crops. Farmers who need to monitor crops in remote areas or who have large fields may find this particularly useful [48, 49]. Irrigation sensors can help farmers save water, protect the environment, and enhance agricultural production.

Fig. (8))
Irrigation sensor.

Sensor Technologies for Monitoring Crop Health

Crop health and condition monitoring is critical for optimizing agricultural practices and guaranteeing optimal production. Sensor technologies are essential for providing real-time crop health indicators data, allowing agriculturalists to make educated choices about irrigation, fertilization, pest management, and other treatments [50, 51]. A number of sensor technologies are used to monitor crop health, including:

Optical Sensors

These sensors use light to assess crop health, such as chlorophyll concentration, which is an important indication of crop health and yield.

Spectral Sensors

These sensors monitor crop spectrum reflectance, providing significant information about crop health and yield.

Fig. (9))
Using optical sensor for detecting plant health.
Fig. (10))
Multi Spectral Sensor [97].

Multispectral and Hyperspectral Imaging

These sensors take pictures of crops using a mix of visible and near-infrared light, giving crucial data on crop health, such as plant stress and nutritional deficits.

Fig. (11))
Multispectral and hyperspectral imaging [96].

Thermal Imaging

These sensors assess crop temperature, providing significant information about crop health and yield.

Fig. (12))
Thermal sensor in action.

Drone-based Sensors

Drones using sensors, such as cameras and spectrometers, may capture airborne photos and data on crop health indicators, such as chlorophyll content and plant stress, and give vital data on crop health and production.

Fig. (13))
Drone-based sensor [98].

Soil Sensors

These sensors assess soil health parameters such as moisture content, temperature, and nutrient levels, providing significant information on crop health and yield. Sensor technologies are important in crop health monitoring because they provide useful data and insights into the factors that affect crop development and overall agricultural operations. Farmers may upsurge the efficiency, effectiveness, and sustainability of their processes by selecting the correct sensors [52].

Fig. (14))
Soil sensor.

IOT-BASED DATA COLLECTION AND MANAGEMENT

The emergence of the Internet of Things (IoT) has revolutionized the gathering, handling, and use of agricultural data. Real-time data retrieval from linked technologies and measuring equipment is made possible by IoT-based systems, which promotes better farming practices, more agricultural production, and informed decision-making. Gathering, managing, and analyzing data from several sources, such as sensors and weather stations, is the fundamental goal of IoT-based smart agricultural systems. This process yields insightful information that helps decision-makers make well-informed choices [53]. The first stage of IoT-based data collecting is called a sensor network, which is made up of wireless sensors positioned throughout fields to collect data on temperature, light intensity, humidity, soil moisture, and nutrient levels. This allows for a complete picture of the agricultural environment.

Overview of Data Collection and Management Systems

In IoT-based smart agriculture systems, data gathering and administration systems are crucial. These systems' purpose is to assemble data from a range of sources, including sensors, weather stations, and drones, and to store and analyze data in a way that delivers important insights and enables informed decision-making.

Data gathering and management systems are classified into two types: centralized and decentralized [54].

Centralized Data Collection and Management Systems

A centralized system collects and stores all data in a single area, such as a cloud-based server. This allows all data to be processed and examined centralized, offering significant insights into the circumstances affecting crop development and overall agricultural operations.

Decentralized Data Collection and Management Systems

In a decentralized system, data is gathered and stored locally at edge devices like weather stations, drones, and sensors. This allows for real-time analysis and insights into the factors driving crop development and overall agricultural operations. The specific requirements of the agriculture industry determine which decentralized or centralized solution is best. Important elements of data gathering, administration, and IoT-based smart agricultural systems offer critical insights that help farmers improve long-term sustainability, productivity, and economic viability.

Cloud-based Data Management Systems

Cloud-based solutions, which are widely used in agriculture, offer scalable infrastructure for managing Internet of Things data and come with benefits, including cost-effectiveness, scalability, accessibility, and sophisticated analytics [55]. Farmers can obtain important insights into the variables influencing crop development and overall operations by processing, storing, and analyzing large amounts of data in an effective manner [55]. These systems act as centralized repositories with infinite capacity, enabling simple access to data at any time and from any place [56]. They provide cost-effective real-time deployment for maximizing output, profitability, and sustainability through data-driven decision-making, thanks to their scalable accessibility and strong security.

Edge Computing in IoT-based Agriculture

Edge computing, a revolutionary approach for IoT-based agriculture, processes data near the source, improving latency and decision-making based on real-time data for farmers [57]. By addressing issues like limited network capacity, connectivity challenges, and privacy concerns, this decentralized approach enhances overall efficiency, reliability, and security in remote or resource-constrained agricultural settings. In IoT-based agriculture, edge computing involves processing data from sensors, drones, and other devices at the network's edge, empowering farmers to boost efficiency, profitability, and sustainability [58].

IOT-BASED PRECISION AGRICULTURE

Precision agriculture, driven by the Internet of Things (IoT), optimizes practices and boosts production by collecting real-time data on climate, soil moisture, crop development, and insect infestations through IoT devices, sensors, and connectivity [16, 17]. Analyzed using powerful analytics and machine learning, this data provides actionable insights for precise decision-making, resulting in optimized resource allocation, reduced input waste, improved crop health monitoring, increased yields, and environmentally friendly farming practices. Transforming traditional techniques, IoT-based precision agriculture contributes to building a more productive and sustainable agricultural business [26, 49].

Definition of Precision Agriculture

Precision agriculture is a data-driven agricultural technique that uses technology to optimize crop yield and reduce waste. Precision agriculture collects data on numerous elements of crop development and management, such as soil fertility, crop yield, and weather patterns, using a range of technologies such as sensors, GPS, drones, and other agricultural equipment. After gathering the information, it is analyzed to produce more informed crop management decisions., such as which seeds to plant, how much water to use, and when to apply fertilizers and pesticides. Precision agriculture aims to boost efficiency, improve yields, and minimize waste, ultimately leading to increased profitability for farmers and more environmental sustainability [16, 17, 59].

Advantages of Precision Agriculture

Precision agriculture has various advantages over traditional farming methods, including:

Enhanced efficiency: Precision agriculture helps farmers make more educated crop management decisions, resulting in enhanced efficiency and yields.

Optimum use of Resources: Precision agriculture helps agriculturalists to optimize The utilization of natural assets like water, fertilizers, and pesticides, minimizing waste and enhancing overall sustainability.

Better decision-making: Precision agriculture gives farmers a more complete picture of the circumstances that affect crop development, allowing for more informed decisions.

Increased profitability: Precision agriculture allows farmers to enhance yields while decreasing waste, resulting in increased earnings.

Better crop growth comprehension: Precision agriculture gives farmers a better understanding of the elements that influence crop growth, allowing them to make more educated crop management decisions.

Improved soil management: Precision agriculture helps farmers get a healthier understanding of their soil's composition and fertility, resulting in better soil management and less soil degradation.

Precision agriculture allows farmers to monitor crop health and discover annoyances and illnesses early, allowing them to take action to evade or limit the spread of these risks. Precision agriculture has various advantages for farmers and the environment, such as enhanced efficiency, better resource management, better decision-making, and more profitability. Precision agriculture has the potential to revolutionize agriculture and add to a more sustainable future by using the latest technological breakthroughs [26, 39, 59].

Real-time Monitoring and Control using IoT in Precision Agriculture

In precision agriculture, real-time control and monitoring based on IoT is based on using sensors and skills to collect information on crop growth and environmentally friendly conditions. This data is then used to make timely crop management decisions to optimize output, reduce waste, and increase efficiency [59, 60]. IoT sensors gather information on a variety of factors, such as soil moisture, temperature, and light intensity, which is then transmitted to a processing hub. For instance, if soil moisture is low, the system may trigger irrigation to alleviate crop stress [39, 49]. Additionally, IoT enables monitoring of agricultural health, pest identification, and crop yield measurement, facilitating informed decisions on fertilization, pest control, and other crop management strategies.

IoT-based real-time monitoring and control in precision agriculture offers increased efficiency by enabling data-driven decisions, reducing resource wastage, and enhancing agricultural yields. Remote monitoring allows farmers to regulate crops from any location, benefiting large-scale operations. Overall, IoT in precision agriculture is a significant advancement that revolutionizes farming and supports a more sustainable future [39, 60].

Using information technology (IT), precision agriculture ensures that soil and crops receive the exact nutrients they need for optimum health and productivity. Profitability, sustainability, and environmental preservation are the goals of precision farming. Satellite farming and site-specific crop management are other names for precision farming. Aside from the aforementioned, there have been significant improvements in agricultural practices and procedures. The modern farmer has shifted away from conventional farming practices and towards more cutting-edge methods. The tabled comparison of the relevant literature is provided below.

IOT-BASED SUSTAINABLE CROP MANAGEMENT

IoT-based sustainable crop management optimizes yields, resource utilization, and environmental impact by employing IoT technology and sensors for real-time data collection and analysis. This enables informed decision-making for maximizing agricultural efficiency, yield, and resource use. Continuous tracking of variables like soil moisture, temperature, and sunlight allows prompt actions such as automated irrigation to address crop stress [75]. IoT facilitates monitoring of agricultural health, pest identification, crop yield measurement, and environmental variables for informed fertilization and pest control decisions, benefiting farmers and the environment with enhanced efficiency, resource management, decision-making, and profitability, contributing to a sustainable future [74, 75].

Crop Monitoring using IoT

Crop monitoring IoT, utilizing devices like drones, actuators, and sensors, collects real-time data on various crop growth and health factors, sending it to a central hub or cloud platform for processing. This information assists farmers in making informed decisions on irrigation, fertilization, pest control, and other treatments to maximize crop production and quality while optimizing resource utilization [76]. IoT-based crop monitoring aims to optimize agricultural yield, minimize waste, and enhance efficiency [24].

Automated Irrigation Systems using IoT

IoT-powered automated irrigation optimizes agriculture using sensors and controllers to make autonomous irrigation decisions based on real-time data, improving efficiency, accuracy, and sustainability. This system reduces waste, enhances output, and allows flexible remote control, saving time and effort for farmers [20, 21, 77].

There are various approaches for using IoT in agriculture to automate irrigation systems. Here are several ways that are regularly used:

Soil Moisture-Based Irrigation: IoT sensors detect soil moisture levels, continually monitoring and sending data to a central hub. Automated irrigation is triggered when soil moisture falls below a set threshold, providing appropriate watering without waste.

IoT-enabled Weather Sensors: Real-time weather data, including temperature, humidity, rainfall, and evapotranspiration rates, is collected. This information calculates crop water requirements and adjusts the irrigation system accordingly.

Crop-Specific Irrigation: IoT-based solutions consider individual water needs of different crops and adjust irrigation plans accordingly, optimizing water supply.

Wireless Valve Control: IoT valve controllers regulate water flow and are remotely controlled based on real-time data and crop requirements, enabling precise irrigation management.

Data Analytics and Decision Support: IoT-based arrangements produce massive data on soil moistness, climate, and crop development. Data analytics and machine learning help agriculturalists make knowledgeable choices, optimizing irrigation and resource management.

These IoT-enabled automated irrigation solutions allow farmers to optimize water consumption, increase crop output, and minimize water waste. These technologies contribute to more efficient and sustainable agricultural practices by combining real-time data, automation, and intelligent decision-making [49, 77].

With the help of these sensors, IoT-enabled automated irrigation systems may offer agriculturalists real-time info about their harvests and the environment, allowing them to optimize watering and decrease waste. If soil moisture levels are acceptable, for example, the system can automatically reduce or stop watering, saving water and energy.

Pest and Disease Management using IoT

IoT in agriculture utilizes technology to monitor pests and diseases. Sensors collect data on environmental conditions, insect populations, and disease occurrences, sending it to a central hub for analysis. Advanced analytics algorithms provide real-time warnings about infestations or outbreaks, enabling precise treatments like smart traps and automated sprayers [78, 79]. This approach offers early identification, targeted treatments, reduced chemical usage, higher crop output, improved quality, and more precise and sustainable pest control, minimizing environmental impact [79]. There are numerous techniques of pest and disease control in agriculture that use IoT [33, 79]. Here are several ways that are regularly used:

Monitoring and Early Detection: IoT sensors monitor eco-friendly factors like temperature, moistness, and light strength, detecting pests through methods like pheromone traps. Farmers receive real-time warnings when pest levels exceed a threshold, enabling prompt preventive actions.

Precision Pesticide Application: IoT-based solutions enable precise and targeted pesticide application. Sensors and data analytics detect affected areas, activating autonomous sprayers or drones for localized pesticide use, reducing chemical consumption and environmental impact.

Biological Control and Integrated Pest Management (IPM): The IoT enhances the deployment of biological control and IPM tactics. Beneficial insects or organisms can be tracked and released via IoT devices, balancing ecosystems and controlling pest populations. Sensor data guides the timing and placement of chemical discharges for effective pest control with reduced pesticide consumption.

IoT systems provide extensive data on environmental conditions, insect numbers, and disease incidences, analyzed through modern analytics to offer insights into pest and disease patterns. Enabled by IoT, smart traps and monitoring devices detect pest activity in real-time, aiding farmers in adopting appropriate pest management strategies. This IoT-based approach helps increase crop yields, make informed decisions, and minimize environmental and agricultural damage caused by pests and diseases.

Literature Review of Sustainable Crop Management

Sustainable crop management is an important part of contemporary agriculture that tries to maximize crop yields while optimizing resource utilization and minimizing environmental damage. Sustainable agricultural management based on IoT offers considerable benefits in terms of optimizing resource utilization, increasing crop production, and reducing environmental impact. Farmers may make educated decisions about irrigation, fertilizer management, pest control, and other farming practizes by utilizing real-time data, advanced analytics, and decision support systems. The incorporation of IoT technology into crop management facilitates the move to precision agriculture, which contributes to more sustainable and efficient food production [74, 75, 80]. CHALLENGES AND FUTURE OF IOT FOR PRECISION AGRICULTURE AND SUSTAINABLE AGRICULTURE

The Internet of Things (IoT) holds immense promise for revolutionizing agriculture, but it faces hurdles that must be addressed [85]. The absence of dependable and inexpensive internet access in rural agricultural areas is a major issue. Interoperability and standardization of IoT devices and platforms also pose difficulties. Data management and privacy are crucial concerns due to the massive amounts of data produced by IoT devices [85]. Additionally, the cost of IoT devices and infrastructure remains a barrier for small-scale farmers [85]. However, advancements in connectivity, data analytics, and AI offer potential improvements for IoT in agriculture [86]. Integration with other technologies like drones and robots can further transform farming practices [86]. Overcoming these obstacles and embracing future advancements will prepare the ground for a more effective, sustainable, and productive agriculture industry [87]. The Internet of Things (IoT) has enormous promise to improve agriculture, but it also confronts significant hurdles.

Challenges for Precision Agriculture and Sustainable Agriculture

The problems and prospects of IoT in agriculture are complicated and ever-changing [85]. Some of the major barriers to IoT application in agriculture include:

Cost: Implementing IoT technology can be costly, particularly for small farmers who might not have the resources to invest in these systems.

Technical Competence: To deploy and maintain IoT systems, which may not be readily available in certain rural communities, technical expertise and skills are required.

Interoperability: To provide significant insights, the various IoT devices and systems used in agriculture must be able to connect and share data effectively.

Data Privacy and Security: We need to protect the enormous amounts of data that IoT devices generate to prevent sensitive information from being leaked or misused.

The next decade of IoT in agriculture promises to revolutionize management through data-driven decisions and real-time optimization [87]. Advancements in AI and machine learning will deepen insights from IoT-generated data, while robotics, including autonomous vehicles and drones, will enhance efficiency and safety. Continuous innovation in these areas will shape the future of IoT in agriculture, benefiting farmers and society as a whole.

Technical Challenges in IoT-based Agriculture

Implementing IoT in agriculture faces challenges, particularly in sensor integration, which involves deploying numerous sensors and ensuring interoperability. Standardizing sensor interfaces and data formats can mitigate this issue. Data management is another challenge, given the massive amount of data generated by IoT. Efficient processing requires scalable data storage, cloud computing resources, and advanced analytics. Reliable and secure internet connectivity in rural agricultural areas is also a technological hurdle for real-time data transmission, requiring increased network coverage and infrastructure strengthening for smooth IoT adoption [85].

There are a number of technological hurdles in using IoT in agriculture [86]. Among the major challenges are:

Interoperability: Ensuring that disparate IoT devices and systems can properly connect and exchange data is a significant concern in agriculture. Differences in communication protocols, data formats, and security standards can all cause interoperability problems.

Data Management: IoT devices produce massive quantities of data in agriculture, which must be adequately stored, processed, and analyzed. This necessitates the use of strong data management systems capable of handling the volume, velocity, and variety of agricultural data.

Network Connectivity: It might be difficult to maintain stable network connectivity in isolated and rural places. This can have an influence on the efficacy of IoT systems and may need the installation of extra infrastructure such as cellular networks, Wi-Fi, or satellite connectivity.

Power Management: IoT devices in agriculture must be built to be energy-efficient and capable of operating in tough environments. This necessitates a thorough examination of power management systems and battery technology.

Device Durability: To resist the extreme weather conditions faced in agricultural activities, IoT devices in agriculture must be tough and sturdy. This necessitates the creation of tough gadgets that can tolerate water, dust, and severe temperatures.

Data Privacy and Security: The huge amount of data must be safeguarded against unauthorized access as well as cyber threats. To maintain the privacy and honesty of agricultural data, sophisticated security measures such as encryption, firewalls, and access restrictions are required.

Addressing these technological issues is important to the effective use of IoT in farming and realising these technologies' full promise for the industry.

Privacy and Security Concerns in IoT-based Agriculture

Implementing IoT in agriculture raises privacy and security concerns due to the massive data collection. Ensuring transparent and secure data acquisition, complying with privacy standards, is crucial. The networked nature of IoT devices introduces vulnerabilities, necessitating measures like access restrictions, authorization, encryption, and safety inspections to defend against cyber threats. Strong security frameworks, stakeholder participation, and industry standards are essential for addressing privacy and security issues and building confidence in these technologies [88].

Several privacy and security risks are related with the usage of IoT in agriculture, including:

Data Security: Agricultural IoT devices capture and retain subtle data such as farm locations, harvest incomes, and livestock health. To guarantee the privacy of farmers and their businesses, this data must be secured against unauthorised access and breaches.

Cybersecurity Risks: Agriculture IoT equipment are subject to cyber-attacks such as hacking, malware, and denial of service (DoS) assaults. This has the potential to impair the effectiveness of IoT equipment and jeopardize the security of important agricultural data.

Unauthorized Access: Without the farmer's permission, unauthorized persons or organizations may get access to IoT devices and systems in agriculture. This can jeopardize agricultural enterprises' privacy and security.

Data Ownership: There are concerns regarding who owns the data created by agricultural IoT devices and how it can be utilized. This encompasses concerns like data sharing, licensing, and data security.

Data Accuracy: IoT devices in agriculture create a great quantity of data; however, this data may be erroneous, partial, or untrustworthy. The efficiency of IoT systems and agricultural decision-making may be impacted by this.

To address these privacy and security problems, it is critical to adopt strong privacy and security measures such as encryption, firewalls, and access restrictions, as well as to develop clear laws and regulations governing data privacy and security in agriculture.

Future Trends in IoT-based Agriculture

AI and ML algorithms integrated with IoT systems provide actionable insights and predictive analytics for efficient crop management [58, 90]. Edge computing reduces latency in remote farming areas, while drones and robotics assist in monitoring crop health and automation tasks based on IoT data [89, 92]. A connected ecosystem promotes transparency and sustainability. Blockchain technology ensures secure and decentralized storage of agriculture data [91, 93]. IoT-based farm management tools enable real-time data access, remote monitoring, and control of agricultural activities [94]. IoT in agriculture has vast potential to revolutionize practices, improve production, sustainability, and efficiency, and focus on global food challenges [95]. The industry awaits fascinating transformations through IoT technology in the coming years.

CONCLUSION

IoT-based smart agriculture solutions have changed how farmers manage their crops and keep an eye on their livestock. Precision agriculture, real-time monitoring and control, automated irrigation and feed management, and enhanced supply chain management have all been made possible by the incorporation of IoT technology like as sensors and data management systems. While there are still technical and privacy problems to be addressed, the future of IoT in agriculture appears bright with continued technological improvements and advancements. Implementing IoT-based smart agricultural systems is critical for assuring sustainable and efficient food production to meet the needs of the world's rapidly expanding population. Precision agriculture and automated feed management are also possible due to IoT, resulting in increased food output. Despite technological and security issues, IoT-based smart agriculture has a bright future and is critical for food production sustainability. The Internet of Things (IoT) has enormous potential to revolutionize farming and increase farming efficiency and sustainability. Furthermore, IoT can enable precision agriculture, allowing farmers to customize their practices to specific crops and even acres. This method avoids waste and decreases environmental effects by using resources just where they are required, which can lead to enhanced soil health over time. Farmers may receive important insights into their crops and farm operations by employing IoT devices and technology, resulting in better efficiency, less waste, and improved sustainability. The rising usage of IoT in agriculture may result in the creation of new employment prospects in sectors like software development, data analytics, and hardware engineering. This can assist in encouraging economic growth and alleviating rural unemployment challenges. IoT has a significant impact on society and agriculture overall, and it has the ability to change how we produce, distribute, and consume food, leading to a more sustainable and effective food system.

Scientific Integrated Solid Waste Management System to Minimize Adverse Effects on Agriculture



Megha Jain1, *, Dhiraj Pandey1
1 J.S.S. Academy of Technical Education, Noida, India

Abstract

The management of solid waste is a major issue everywhere in the world. It is an undesirable material produced from industrial and business activities andfrom residential areas in a given region, which leads to adverse impacts on agriculture. Decentralized municipal solid waste is one of the causes of the harmful environment in India and all over the world. Decentralized waste management causes significant problems, including hazardous diseases and environmental pollution. To tackle these problems, scientific management of waste disposal is needed. Few methods are used to find the solution to these problems, but they do not give precise and accurate results. Moreover, there is uncertainty in data, so there is an immediate need to establish a reliable way to find a place where solid waste can be disposed of. A scientific, integrated solid waste management system must be immediately designed to reduce the effects on agriculture. Geospatial tools like Remote Sensing and GIS, which can help in appropriate site choice for Municipal Solid Waste (MSW) disposal in an additional scientific manner, might result in economically supported concrete proof. The study provides the suitable places in the city and the best algorithms in the field of Site Suitability Analysis (SSA).

Keywords: Composition, Human excreta, Integrated solid waste management system, Nutrient recovery, Recycling, Site suitability analysis, Waste disposal.

* Corresponding author Megha Jain: J.S.S. Academy of Technical Education, Noida, India; Tel: 9999772710; E-mail: meghajain12@gmail.com

INTRODUCTION

Most urban regions in the nation are tormented by intense issues identified with solid waste because of the constant movement of individuals from provincial and semi-urban territories to towns and urban areas.

The world population is increasing rapidly with the rise of economic growth, and a high standard of living increases solid waste all over the world. Because of the lack of information, it is recorded that a high quantity of solid waste is dumped in

water bodies in open ground areas, which is the most common practice of the municipalities [1].

The drainage system is the pattern formed by streams and rivers over the landform. It is also known as the river system. Most of the daily water requirements are fulfilled by these river systems of an area, so it is very necessary to avoid these patterns from the waste management sites [2].

This decentralized waste then causes environmental degradation. Non-scientific disposal of waste leads to hazardous diseases in society and at the community level. Mismanagement and improper planning by the authorities lead to the non-scientific disposal of waste. For sustainable agriculture in a country, it is vital to employ a management strategy regarding modern approaches to solid waste disposal. Scientific ways of waste disposal will affect in a way that there will be no harm to any agriculture field or the environment [3].

A methodology and an appropriate location for disposing of solid waste are suggested in this research study [4]. This methodology will help to centralize all the solid trash in a good scientific manner without harming agriculture, and it is also one of the more affordable options. Satellite remotely sensed data and geospatial technology are used to solve the solid waste disposal location suitability problem [5].

The article has six sections. The related work on integrated solid waste management is studied. After that, we define the problem and find the solution to solve the solid waste disposal location suitability problem, and then we explain the methodology to design a scientifically integrated solid waste management system. This article summarizes the technical hurdles in results and implementation. Lastly, it compares and concludes the article.

RELATED STUDY

The multi-criteria decision-making approach for site appropriateness analysis in a fuzzy environment is the main emphasis of this study. Waste disposal practices cause the decline of agricultural land. A scientifically integrated solid waste management system must be studied to reduce adverse environmental effects.

García et al. explainedthat the chemicals in wastewater have a significant impact on the agricultural reuse of municipal wastewater. Additionally, due to the presence of pathogens, environmental risk and the influence on human health are the main issues with municipal wastewater reuse [6].

Akram Qazi et al.explained that traditional wastewater systems are made up of a combination of chemical and biological processes that remove organic matter and nutrients from wastewater, resulting in a high removal efficiency of BOD, total nitrogen, and phosphorus [7].

Aquino et al.found that instead of being dumped into the environment, both treated and untreated wastewater can be used to irrigate crops. Pathogen threats, on the other hand, must be completely addressed to maintain food safety because agriculture consumes over 70% of the world's water [8].

Cherif et al. suggested that pollutant removal procedures in hydroponic systems use a combination of physical, biological, and chemical activities with microorganisms, plants, and media-based interactions [9].

C. Namasivayam and K. Kadirvelu suggested that field-grown plants get the majority of their nutrients from the earth, and nutrient formulations for field cultivation are considerably different from those used for hydroponic crop production [10].

Vaverková et al. explained that agricultural reuse of wastewater primarily occurrs following the use of several types of technology, including primary treatment and biological treatment systems. Primarily processed home wastewater was used [11]

Increases in crop growth have been observed in the literature when crops are irrigated with treated municipal wastewater. In this regard, various studies have shown that treated effluents can boost crop growth and yield, such as wheat [12].

PROBLEM STATEMENT

In the literature survey, few methods are used to find the solution to this problem, but they do not give precise and accurate results. Also, there is uncertainty in data, so there is an immediate need to find a suitable method to find the place for the disposal of waste. Decentralized municipal solid waste is one of India's agricultural problems, and decentralized waste management causes environmental pollution. To tackle this problem, waste management must be carried out scientifically. Geospatial tools like Remote Sensing and GIS can help in appropriate site choice for solid waste disposal. Remote Sensing and GIS techniques can efficiently find suitable sites for waste disposal with effective low cost.

METHODOLOGY

Studies reveal that these open dumps of solid waste cause a harmful impact on agriculture. A non-scientific disposal system of solid waste leads to a downturn in agricultural land. The management of municipal solid waste in many Asian nations remains a significant challenge, not only due to environmental and aesthetic concerns but also because of the large volumes generated daily. To minimize adverse effects on the environment, we have to design a scientifically integrated solid waste management system.

Geospatial tools like Remote Sensing and GIS, which can help in appropriate site choice for MSW disposal in an additional scientific manner, might result in economically supported concrete proof. In this study, six layers, i.e., Slope, Geomorphology, Drainage, Soil, Lithology, and LULC pattern, of the study area were generated from different sources using GIS techniques. A Pairwise Comparison Matrix was prepared according to the importance of each layer considering the issue. Comparison is made between the three approaches - F-AHP, F-TOPSIS, and Compromise Programming with ranking methods for calculating weightage for different criteria.

The management of solid waste in agricultural soils is an important component of sustainable agricultural practices. GIS (Geographic Information System) approaches for site appropriateness study can provide useful insights for effective solid waste management in agricultural settings. This method entails combining spatial data on soil qualities, land usage, waste generation, and trash disposal alternatives to determine ideal places for waste management in agricultural fields. Here is an overview of how GIS techniques can be utilized in solid waste management for site appropriateness analysis:

Data collection: Soil maps, land use/land cover data, topography data, trash creation rates, waste composition, and prospective waste disposal alternatives are all examples of relevant data sets. These statistics are available from a variety of sources, including government organizations, research institutes, and field surveys.

Data Integration: The gathered data is imported into a GIS software platform. This stage entails geo-referencing the data and ensuring that multiple datasets are compatible. GIS software, such as ArcGIS or QGIS, has data integration and manipulation tools.

Site Suitability Criteria: Site suitability criteria are based on agricultural soil requirements, waste characteristics, and environmental standards. Soil type, soil fertility, nutrient content, organic matter content, waste composition, proximity to water bodies, slope, and land use constraints are all important aspects to consider.

Overlay Analysis and Weighting: Weights are assigned to each criterion based on its relative value in the site selection process. Weighting can be subjective or objective, and it can be based on specialist knowledge. Overlay analysis should be used to combine these weighted criteria and provide a composite suitability map. This map will show regions that match the waste application criterion in agricultural soils.

Validation and Field Verification: Field surveys and sample collection are conducted to validate the results of the GIS analysis. This stage ensures that the chosen sites are truly suitable for waste application and are meeting local rules. Field verification also enables the site appropriateness model to be adjusted and fine-tuned.

Monitoring and Management: A monitoring system is developed to evaluate the effects of trash application on soil quality, crop development, and environmental conditions. Regular monitoring aids in regulating waste application rates, addressing any concerns, and preserving the agricultural system's long-term sustainability.

Public Awareness and Participation: Stakeholders are involved in the decision-making process, such as farmers, waste management authorities, and local communities. Raising knowledge about the advantages of employing GIS tools for site appropriateness assessments and encouraging engagement in waste management practices are two ways to get people involved.

Solid waste in agricultural soils can be managed more efficiently by using GIS tools for site appropriateness evaluations. This method aids in the optimization of waste application, the reduction of environmental dangers, and the promotion of sustainable farming practices.

As shown in Fig. (1). the comparative geospatial analysis of F-AHP and CP algorithms show almost similar results, thus, both algorithms are capable of generating a suitable result. Color composites are of three types. First is natural color composites i.e., area units fabricated from the inexperienced, blue, and red part of the spectrum. This leads to a picture with realistic colors. The second pseudo-natural color composites i.e., color composites, are made with different elements of the spectrum; however, the result has natural wanting colors, and the third type is false-color composites. In false color composites, the colors within the image are not constant as in reality; for example, healthy vegetation is red, and concrete area units are blue [13].

Fig. (1))
Remote Sensing Process [3].

Every object has different spectral reflectance characteristics. The tone or color of an object depends on its reflectance characteristics. Forests appear green because they reflect green wavelength. Objects captured by the sensors can be distinguished based on the recorded or measured spectral signatures, which depend on wavelength.

Stages in Remote Sensing

• The first stage is the emission of EMR.

• The transmission of energy from the earth's surface to its energy source, as well as its absorption and scattering.

• Reflection and emission.

• Energy transmission from the surface to the remote sensor.

• Output of data and result.

• Information transmission and handling.

Resolution

The system can render data at the smallest distance-separable quantity, the EMR time wavelength band. Figure. 2 illustrates the resolution. Satellites can capture images from l km to 10 m distance with a resolution.

Fig. (2))
Resolution.

Resolutions of Remote Sensing

There are two resolutions of remote sensing: spectral and radiometric proportion.

Spectral: Spectral analysis involves utilizing a proportion of the width of the frequency spectrum (data transfer capacity) to produce the image, enabling detailed examination of different wavelengths for more comprehensive data interpretation. The smaller the data transfer capacity, the higher the spectral resolution. The finer the spectral resolution, the narrower the wavelength varies for a selected channel or band.

Radiometric: It is a measure of the minimum change in the radiance, which can be measured by the temporal frequency of observation. This frequency is defined by the interval in days between two consecutive observations of a specific ground target under consistent viewing geometry.Geographic Information System By taking into account all the necessary parameters, geographic information system techniques are applied to provide a superior land. A GIS is a geographic information system (GIS), which is a type of database management system used to execute suitability models for actual-world scenarios and handle massive volumes of spatially dispersed data from many sources, as illustrated in Fig. (3). This is a fundamental requirement for the appropriate disposal of garbage generated within the area. It is the procedure through which garbage is gathered and disposed of in the appropriate location [14].

Fig. (3))
GIS in the real world [4].

Criteria for extracting the data

In the proposed research process of extracting the data, data was generated from different sources and analyzed thoroughly. Majorly, six criteria (i) soil, (ii) slope, (iii) geomorphology, (iv) lithology, (v) law use cover, and (iv) drainage are considered to determine the suitable location. The sequence of this approach is divided into four phases.

Phase- 1: Extraction and digitization of satellite data.

Phase -2: Reclassification of the layer.

Phase- 3: Data standardization and weight estimation using fuzzy- AHP.

Phase- 4: Applying algorithms, data classification and comparative analysis

In the first phase, satellite imagery is used to create various criteria for this research study. Six major criteria layers were extracted using Indian remote-sensing Satellite-P6, Cartosat-1, CartoDem, and Sentinel data. Majorly, six criteria are considered important aspects to determine the suitable location. These layers are the dataset for the proposed research. The different criteria were studied separately and according to the need for a landfill site. Each criterion is divided into sub-categories, and then each sub-category is further classified according to its suitability regarding the issue being considered. The sub-categories range from I to 10 according to their suitability, where 2 is the lowest and 10 is the highest priority. All the layers were reclassified, and the maps were standardized, with the weights estimated using different estimation procedures. Finally, respective chains of algorithms were applied to get the final map with suitable sites, applying the MCI algorithm over the data in a fuzzy environment and using these datasets to identify the highly suitable site for solid waste disposal using multi-resolution satellite images. All the intermediate datasets were modulated to obtain outputs using geoprocessing tools. A brief illustration of the methodology is depicted in Fig. (4).

A. Data and Material

Data and materials are obtained from different sources. The data is further divided into two sections: primary and secondary data. The primary material includes the interpretation of remote sensing satellite data by visual investigation [15]. The secondary data are obtained from different sectors of government bodies like the census department SoI (Survey of India), Nagar Nigam, Geological Department, and Soil and Land Use Planning Department of India. Satellite remote sensing data was obtained from Bhuvan Geoportal of the NRSC (National Remote Sensing Centre), Hyderabad.

The research study provides the best identical area and appropriate site for waste disposal, and further study will help us identify which algorithm is best for precision calculation and site suitability for agriculture. The six spatial layers were standardized and further subcategorized. Appropriate scores were given to individual layers ranging from 2 to 10. The different criteria were studied separately and according to the need for a landfill site for agriculture. Each criterion was divided into sub-categories, and then each sub-category was further classified according to its suitability regarding the issue being considered [16].

Fig. (4))
Proposed Methodology.

IMPLEMENTATION AND RESULTS

Site suitability analysis is a procedure of selecting a suitable site considering all qualities and factors regarding the purpose for which analysis is being performed. It entails a thorough analysis of the natural resources and processes that define a location, incorporating mapping techniques and GIS technologies to interpret the geographic data [17].

Detail analysis and research of different maps and data like slope, soil type, land use, lithology, geomorphology, drainage, or any other criteria relevant to the purpose that is being considered are required, as illustrated in Fig. (5) [18]. Different layers were generated using different sources; LULC map was generated through supervised classification of the data provided by sentinel satellite, slope map, and drainage map were prepared using DEM files downloaded from BHUVAN (NRSC) and lithology, geomorphology, and soil map were digitized [19].

Fig. (5))
Integration of suitability criteria with fuzzy algorithm.

The process of selecting these suitable sites manually is very time-consuming, so in this case, using GIS and remote sensing as a tool helps us a lot and gives us accurate results, saving our time and effort [20].

Simple Additive Weighting (SAW) Technique

The map shown in Fig. (6) demonstrates the area of a suitable site for solid waste disposal, which is categorized into four parts. This map is generated by integrating geospatial tools and SAW algorithms in a GIS environment. Statistics data are shown in describing the distribution of regions concerning highly and moderately suitable and non-suitable sites for solid waste [21].

Fig. (6))
Suitable area for solid waste disposal using the SAW method.

Compromise Programming Technique

The suitability map shown in Fig. (7) demonstrates the area of suitable site for solid waste disposal, which is categorized into four parts. This map is generated by integrating geospatial tools and compromise programming algorithms in the GIS environment to describe the distribution of regions concerning highly and moderately suitable and unsuitable sites for solid waste [22].

Fig. (7))
Suitable area for solid waste disposal using compromise programming method.

TOPSIS Technique

TOPSIS technique is one of the multicriteria decision-making (MCDM) methods. It is a ranking technique [23]that provides each quantitative and qualitative study of the matter. The TOPSIS technique offers a straightforward and rapid approach to addressing real-life issues. To ensure consistency in the results obtained by the AHP technique, the TOPSIS technique is also applied [24].

The projected MCDM methodology has the advantage that it will overcome the drawbacks of the prevailing ways. In Fig. (8), a statistical analysis graph shows that the I -TOPSIS provides a large area of a highly suitable region as compared to SAW and compromise programming [25].

Fig. (8))
Comparative analysis for SAW, TOPSIS, and CP.

All the algorithms (SAW, CP, and F-TOPS IS) were studied and applied to the datasets. These algorithms are very complex when applied to the real-world problem. For effective decision making, an analytic hierarchy process is used for weight estimation [22].

In response, a comparative analysis between two completely different MCDM ranking approaches, namely, TOPSIS and VIKOR, is conducted to determine their performance in prioritizing the foremost vital product aspects in client reviews. Among various MCDM approaches, TOPSIS and VIKOR are particularly notable. Despite employing different aggregation functions, both strategies effectively identify key product attributes [26].

This article proposes a novel multicriteria decision-making (MCDM) methodology employing the TOPSIS method and similarity measures between fuzzy values. The scientific and mathematical computation of spatial data provided a precise result of suitable sites. A final site suitability map was generated, and an Analysis was done to observe the working of algorithms on all the datasets [27]. To visualize the exact location, final maps were integrated with real-time satellite images, as shown in Fig. (9) [28].

Fig. (9))
Satellite image of a suitable site for solid waste disposal.

After reviewing all three results, two sites are identified as suitable for solid waste disposal. These are the exact eligible locations found for solid waste disposal, and they also meet all of the requirements for being a good site for solid waste disposal [29, 30].

CONCLUSION

The study concludes the effective use of the geospatial multicriteria decision-making technique for site suitability of solid waste disposal in the Dehradun district. The multi-criteria decision-making approach to site suitability analysis in a fuzzy environment is the main emphasis of this study. The site that is highly suitable for the disposal of waste is identified successfully using these fuzzy algorithms. It is observed that simple additive weighting and compromise programming show similar results. It is also observed that F-TOPSIS is very complex in calculating the overall mathematics. The SAW method is very effective in solving such problems, but it is less efficient than compromise programming and F-TOPSIS. In the same manner, compromise programming is less efficient in finding highly suitable sitesthan F-TOPSIS. A statistical comparative analysis graph shows that the F-TOPSIS provides a large area of highly suitable site region as compared to the SAW and compromise programming method. In this research, it is concluded that satellite remote sensing and geospatial technology integrated with complex mathematical algorithms are powerful in finding the appropriate site for waste disposal in the city. This model will help the administration and city planners plan better management for solid waste. This type of scientific research study will be useful in finding suitable sites for government facilities. Our research study can be implemented in all types of site suitability analysis (SSA) problems and help the scientific community understand the implementation of complex mathematical algorithms in geospatial technology.

FUTURE SCOPE

Future studies must be based on the optimization of the site suitability problem by taking more than six criteria. Criteria like road network must be included in future projects. This will optimize the cost of transportation. In this model, generalization is preserved and hence can be used for other sites and locations.
